# Continuity State

## How to read this file, and why the guide is at the top

**This file is a rolling window. It holds the global canon, the whole of the volume currently being written, and nothing older, and everything older is in `state/archive/continuity-volumes-01-08.md`, verbatim, with every item number exactly as it was here.** The window moved for Volumes 01 to 07 at the Volume 07 close and for Volumes 01 to 08 at the Volume 08 close, **and after that cut this file holds the global canon and nothing else, because Volume 09 has not been written yet.** The reason is arithmetic and it is not a preference. As of the review of Batch 0004 this file was **1.94 MB and about 373,000 words, growing about 1,100 words a chapter, which is roughly a third of the manuscript**, and it was being carried whole into every phase, which is the opposite of what AGENTS.md asks a rolling summary to be. It is now about 170,000 characters, and it stays about that size for as long as the window is maintained.

**Three rules make this safe, and a phase that breaks one of them has broken canon rather than tidied it.**

1. **Items are never renumbered.** 1 to 1000 are fixed and are what they were, and the highest of them, item 1000, is the last item of the Volume 08 close. 646 onward were appended by volume, by batch and by repair pass in the order they were established, and that is unchanged. **An item number means the same thing whether you find it here or in the archive**, and the archive carries the same numbers, so *item 412* is findable either way.
2. **Nothing is deleted, only moved.** The archive is a byte-for-byte copy of what was cut, and the four files under `state/archive/` reconstruct any earlier state of any of the four ledgers exactly.
3. **A volume is never archived while it is being read.** The window moves forward at a volume close, after the close has audited the volume, and not before.

**Read this guide, then read the section for the volume you are actually working on, and do not read the whole thing. For the Volume 09 first prompt the useful reading is: this guide, `state/current.md`, `outline/volumes/volume-08-close.md` §§5, 6, 7, 9 and 10, the seventeen threads under *Threads the Volume 08 close hands to Volume 09* in `state/archive/open-threads-volumes-01-08.md`, and items 984 to 1000 in `state/archive/continuity-volumes-01-08.md`. Everything before item 984 is in the archive and is reachable by grep, not by reading.** **The highest item at any time is the newest, and the number is in `state/current.md`.**

**The archive index, so a number can be found without grepping a megabyte.** `state/archive/continuity-volumes-01-08.md` holds, in order and unaltered, everything `continuity-volumes-01-07.md` holds plus all of Volume 08 and the Volume 08 close: the Volume 01 to Volume 05 batch and close sections, the six Volume 06 sections, the Volume 06 close, the Volume 07 batch sections and its close, the five Volume 08 batch sections, their review-repair sections, and the Volume 08 close's own items 984 to 1000. **Items 1 to 645 are Volume 01 to Volume 05 and are fixed. Items 646 to 897 are Volume 06, Volume 07's batches and the Volume 07 close. Items 898 to 983 are the Volume 07 close's tail and the five Volume 08 batch sections. Items 984 to 1000 are the Volume 08 close. The live window is empty and opens on Volume 09.**

**Items that are marked superseded in part are the point of them, and the markers are the point.** Item 746 is a block device ledger whose seven figures did not reproduce on their own stated basis, and item 748 reported a sweep of a block clean when three of the paragraphs it claimed to have repaired were still on the page. Both are left standing as the first printing and both point forward. A writer who needs a Volume 06 Block 4 figure takes it from item 757, and a writer who needs to know what the block believed takes it from 746 and 748 and knows that nine figures in them were wrong.

**One warning that cost a real error. Do not trust a line-number pointer into a chapter file in this ledger.** Every repair pass inserts and removes lines, so a pointer written by one phase is stale by the next one. Item 697 pointed the four lines of the winter book at `chapters/volume-06/chapter-0266.md:39` and the actual positions were 47; after the Batch 0002 review repair they are 45, 47, 49 and 51. **Refer to a passage by its chapter and its opening words instead, and if you must use a line number, re-derive it in the same turn you use it.**

Bootstrap canon is established in `bible/`, `outline/series.md`, and `outline/ending.md`. **Volume 08 is complete and closed; the close record is `outline/volumes/volume-08-close.md` and it is the authority for items 984 to 1000 and for every figure in this project's current state.**

## Hard canon

- World: Neyra, a late-medieval river-and-road world. Years count from the **Quiet Reckoning**; the story opens in **YR 312, Mudmonth**.
- Protagonist: Sir Aren Kest, thirty-two, a former Lantern Guard captain and current bridge repairer in Merefen. Estranged from Tovan; still idealizes Edric.
- Starting mystery: the Bellweather Sundering, **YR 301, Mudmonth**, erased Bellweather and seven villages. The story opens on the eleventh anniversary, in the same season. The Crown blames Aren’s failed bridge defense.
- The seven villages: Alder Cross, Saltbrim, Wrenlow, Cobbett, Nine Elms, Stonebarrow, Hushwater.
- Deeper answer: Marshal Edric Hunn and the Lantern Council ordered a forced cull to contain the Unwritten Wound; the order sacrificed a living city.
- Gatehouse rule: a living path cannot be reset or forcibly closed while conscious life exists inside it. Closing a route removes Aren’s access, not the world.
- **Local custody rule (added in bootstrap):** roads are opened by named local custodians, not by Aren. Two consenting custodians with paired anchors can open a road without him, and path communities can open roads to each other without the main world. Aren is a translator and temporary common anchor, never a necessary mechanism. The ending must show roads working without him.
- Path capacity: one at Stage 0/First Stitch, then two, three, five, seven, and ten at later stages. The final Unowned Road stage removes exclusive personal access.
- Path costs: roadmarks, memory loss, path bleed, lost time, failed workings, social obligations, and permanent local consequences. No path is a disposable map.
- Crossing budget: about three attempted crossings per season at any stage; **three days of fever after a return, at Stage 0 and at Stage 1 alike** — the first crossing in Volume 01 pays the same three days — growing to a month at Stage 6. A return stitch is consumed by use.
- Final enemy: Edric Hunn, using the established First Witness protocol to activate the One Road. No new final enemy is planned.

## Ending constraints

The ending must preserve Bellweather-Living as a living sovereign community, contain but not magically cure the Unwritten Wound, ratify the Charter of Many Roads, keep Aren and Mara’s relationship voluntary and independent, and leave Edric alive for public judgment.

## Knowledge control

`outline/series.md` contains a per-volume table of who knows what. Do not reveal a fact to any character before the volume listed there. This is the primary tool for protecting the central mystery across 900 chapters.

## Canon clarifications recorded in this phase

1. **Name change.** The Culling Order field warden was renamed **Sera Dain → Sabin Dain** to remove a collision with Sera Quen. Use Sabin Dain in all prose and state.
2. **Name change.** The Lantern Council chancellor was renamed **Veyra Senn → Iselde Senn** to remove a collision with Mara Vey.
3. **Cast discipline.** At most six new named characters per batch. Every recurring character needs one want unrelated to Aren. No supporting character is killed for shock.
4. **The bell has provenance.** Crown salvage, sold at a Merefen clearance for two pence, bought by Aren as scrap bronze in the opening Mudmonth. It is a damaged anchor, not a key or a summons.
5. **Mara’s archive is the main-world Bellweather Claims Archive** in Merefen’s salt-loft rooms. It is not the living archive inside Bellweather-Living, which is run by Hollis Dree and refuses to surrender copies.
6. **Relationship policy is decided:** one slow-burn relationship, Aren and Mara Vey. No harem.
7. **Aren’s age arithmetic.** Aren is 32 at the opening and was **21** at the Sundering (born YR 280, apprenticed at twelve, Guard at seventeen, commissioned by brevet at twenty-one). This is deliberate: he was the youngest captain in the Guard, which is why Edric chose him, why the Crown can blame a boy, and why Aren believed his own defence for three years. Do not rewrite the Sundering as a child being made to do it alone. He held a real command and really failed it.
8. **The Rusk families are separated.** Jos Rusk, the Hushwater ferryman, survives and lives in Merefen; his claim is the first one Mara Vey personally refutes, and he is wrong about the cause. His wife **Anwen Rusk** (37, who kept the Hushwater ferry’s crossing book) and daughter **Bryn Rusk** (11) are among the erased and are now named outright instead of being left as “the wife and daughter.” The recurring Merefen ferryman formerly called Pei Rusk is **Pei Tarrow** (52, Hushwater survivor’s son, freight boats on the Old Toll channel). He is no relation to Jos Rusk. Do not stage the two men together, and do not let a bystander call Pei a Rusk.
9. **Hanna Vey and Mara Vey are not related.** The shared surname is deliberate common naming from the Sable river country, not a plot relation, not a mistake, and not a reason to rename either of them later. Let Mara notice the coincidence once, early and coldly, and never return to it. No scene may imply kinship.
10. **Toll figures are locked.** Bellweather city: about **four thousand**. The seven villages: about **seven hundred and twenty** (Alder Cross 200, Saltbrim 90, Wrenlow 60, Cobbett 140, Nine Elms 110, Stonebarrow 40, Hushwater 80). The Crown quotes the city figure alone and folds the villages into it; the Archive keeps the two numbers apart on every page and never prints a combined total. In a book whose moral weight rests on exact arithmetic, a character arguing about the dead must use the number actually being argued about.
11. **Dispatch reality, for the record.** The runner selects the first `workspace/**/PROMPT.md` in sorted order that has no `.done` or `.blocked` marker. `state/phase-ledger.json` and `PHASE_SYSTEM.md` are controller-owned, are never written by the writer, and the ledger is not what dispatch reads — so the only protection against a repeated batch is the prompt files themselves. Consequence: **exactly one prompt may exist per batch.** The duplicate `workspace/phase-002-batch-plan/` prompt, which also asked for Chapters 1–10, was deleted during the phase-000 fix pass. `workspace/volume-01/batch-0001/PROMPT.md` is the single prompt for the first batch and pairs with `outline/batches/volume-01-batch-0001.md`. The `.wip-conflict` marker in `workspace/phase-000-bootstrap/` only gates WIP-branch resume and is cleared by the runner on completion; leave it alone.
12. **The named dead are records, not cast.** Ferris Oat, Hanna Vey, Corin Slate, Bess Alder, Anwen Rusk, and Bryn Rusk are read aloud, quoted, filed, and argued over; they do not act on the page and do not count against the six-new-named-characters-per-batch limit. That limit applies to characters who speak, decide, or obstruct. A survivor or claimant who acts is a character and does count: Jos Rusk and Pei Tarrow are both cast, not records.

## Canon clarifications recorded in the Volume 01 outline phase

13. **The Sundering's date is the nineteenth of Mudmonth.** The story opens on the sixth of Mudmonth, YR 312, so the eleventh anniversary falls thirteen days later, and the storm comes up on the night of the eighteenth into the nineteenth. Volume 01 opens on the sixth of Mudmonth and closes in the last week of Fallowmonth, about five months of main-world time, matching the series time ledger.
14. **Aren's aunt is dead.** She raised him after Ilyra's crossing and died of the low-quarter fever three winters before the opening, so Aren has no household, rents one room above a cooper on Weir Street, and nobody at home has to be told where he is. The person who holds his tools and covers for him is Tovan.
15. **The bell's economics and dates are fixed.** Aren buys the bell on the first of Mudmonth, YR 312, at the Crown clearance for two pence, **five days** before Chapter 1 opens on the sixth. Chapter 2 is the **eleventh**, the day the Merefen bridge guild's removal notice reaches him, so the notice is dated the day it arrives — eight days before the anniversary, and before any storm. The guild's standing valuation is **six marks** as scrap bronze, because the crack is worth the difference and the roll is right. In Chapter 2 Aren cannot raise six marks and signs a guild bond for the sum with his tool-chest as surety. He refuses Tovan's honest six-mark offer and takes the bond instead, on purpose: the metal buys nothing, a bell in a yard belongs to somebody else, and a bond puts the bell in his name in front of the guild. He has chosen to be the man answerable for it, and he never says so to anyone. The hold he signs in Chapter 1 is good until the guild's next sitting, **the twenty-fourth** — and nothing happens on the twenty-fourth, because a bench cannot act against a man who is not in front of it and Aren is nine days gone. That is the town's own process for a cracked bell, and the watch's sealed order replaces it three days later, on the twenty-seventh, without anybody in Merefen getting to choose between them. The bond lapses when the watch takes the bell, which is a smaller humiliation than the market days the closed road costs.
16. **Return stitches cannot be improvised for free.** Aren rings the bell without having prepared a return stitch, which is the mistake that shapes the batch: he is stranded for six path days, loses **nine** days of the main-world calendar against a Gatehouse warning of three, and returns on the twenty-seventh to a bell already seized. A working time-diverge warning in this series is wrong by days or weeks, never silently by months, and the drift is stated in the prose as a warning that was wrong.
17. **The path's own answer comes from two institutions.** The Bellweather Bridge Guild kept the **counterfoil** — the stub proving a severance writ was sent to Bellweather-Living on the nineteenth of Mudmonth, YR 301, refused on the deck of the arch and withdrawn. The **outgoing writ register** naming the signatory is kept by the toll houses, who are the guild's political opponents. The volume midpoint reveal lives in the toll-house counting room, not the guild hall.
18. **Path-side vocabulary is the volume's argument.** Bellweather-Living maintains its crossing through **stewards**; a steward maintains. The corrupted Crown term is **private keeper**, and a provincial document applies it to Aren during Batch 0001. The legitimate word *custodian* is not used in Volume 01. The word "keeper" fitting Aren is a Volume 01–03 hook and is never explained in Volume 01.
19. **Batch 0001's six names are fixed:** Tovan Kest, Mara Vey, Wenna Koll, Lysa Fenn, Captain Rell, Sera Quen — all six already established in `bible/characters.md` with a want, a flaw, and a function, and named in prose for the first time in this batch. Jos Rusk, Bram Ottery, Halven, Pei Tarrow, Sarn Oyelaran, and Ilvane Quarren are Batch 0002. Bram Ottery therefore **does not carry out the bell seizure in Chapters 8–10**: the seizure is Rell's watch, and its sergeant is addressed by office and left unnamed so the six-name cap is not spent on a man who performs one errand. Bram enters by name in Volume 02, when somebody has to answer in writing. Rell's unlawful seizure remains the fact recorded against him. A full batch-by-batch cast plan is in `outline/volume-01.md`.
20. **Batch 0001 uses two POVs plus one.** Aren in eight chapters, Mara in Chapter 3, Tovan in Chapter 9. The path-side institutions in the batch are addressed by office — the bench, the bench-clerk, the hull-master, the toll-house steward — so the six-name cap is not spent on people who only exist to be quoted.
21. **Two Gatehouse lines in the whole first batch,** one at the ringing and one at the return, both plain and both technically true in a way that conceals the moral cost.
22. **Planted in Batch 0001, owed later.** The bell bought for two pence (Chapter 1); the Merefen bridge guild's removal notice **dated the eleventh, the day it arrives** — before the storm and before any crossing (Chapter 2), which pays off when the watch's sealed seizure order turns out to be dated the same week (Chapter 8); Ferris Oat's brine weights, **held and cited but not filed** — the extract Mara trades for his promise (Chapter 3) — with the formal entry of the named roll of the dead reserved for **Chapter 892**, where it is the first document the Charter names; the bell ringing in the anniversary storm (Chapter 4); the empty iron cradle left on the near abutment after the seizure (Chapter 8), which is **not** still empty in Chapter 50 — the town reclaims its own iron during the Block 5 resolution, bands the crack, and the bell returns to the bridge without ever returning to Aren; Aren's deferred field notes and his three years of repeating the official fever account, disclosed in his own hand in Chapter 10, together with the two omissions he files alongside them. These are the Volume 01 setups named in `outline/ending.md`, and the writer must not add a second version of any of them. In particular, the brine weights are a cited authority in Volume 01 and a Chapter 892 payoff; filing them as an exhibit in Chapters 10 or 48 spends the ending's beat early and must not happen.
23. **Volume 01's cast discipline on the Culling Order.** No named member appears in Volume 01. Sabin Dain enters in Volume 2. The phrase "Culling Order" may appear at most once in Volume 01, in a document or a claimant's mouth, with no member attached to it.
24. **Hushwater Reach exists in the path.** Bellweather-Living has a living hamlet on a channel that is drowned in the main world, and its ferry cooperative keeps a crossing book in a living hand with different names in it. The cooperative counts **ferry hands and hearths, not souls**, so nobody can put a number on the hamlet; the best estimate in play is **near sixty**. That is deliberately far from the **eighty** erased at Hushwater in the locked toll figures, because a living hamlet of exactly eighty beside an erased village of exactly eighty is a signpost and this volume is only allowed a hint. No character notices the gap, and no one explains the coincidence in Volume 01. The thread stays held until Volume 09.
25. **The first crossing's calendar is locked, and so is the fever.** Crossing one opens on the night of the eighteenth into the nineteenth of Mudmonth and returns on the **twenty-seventh**; path time inside it is six days. The fever takes **three days — the twenty-seventh, twenty-eighth and twenty-ninth — and is over on the thirtieth**, which is the day Chapter 10 opens. The thirtieth is not a fourth day of illness and must not be called one; write it as the day the fever breaks. Three days is the figure in the crossing budget above and in `outline/volume-01.md`; `outline/ending.md` states no fever length and is not a source for it. The cost does not grow until Stage 6.
26. **The batch's day map is the only calendar the first ten chapters use.** Ch 1 the sixth · Ch 2 the eleventh · Ch 3 the twelfth · Ch 4 the night of the eighteenth into the nineteenth · Ch 5–7 path time with no main-world clock · Ch 8 the twenty-seventh · Ch 9 the twenty-seventh to the thirtieth · Ch 10 the thirtieth. The thirteenth to the eighteenth is a stated cut: no visitor, no document, no arrest, no further bell sound. **The bell stays on its cradle across that cut because of the twenty-fourth hold in item 15, not because a date was missed.** Volume 01's own time budget in `outline/volume-01.md` now lists the same days.
27. **Two guilds, two names, no crossing between them.** "The guild" in the main world is the **Merefen bridge guild**: a town's craft body of bridgewrights and road-wardens holding the Old Toll crossing in municipal trust, keeping the scrap roll the bell's six-mark valuation comes from. The path's institution is always the **Bellweather Bridge Guild**. They never appear in each other's world in Volume 01, and a chapter must not let "the guild" be ambiguous about which one it means.
28. **The promise Aren makes to Mara is undated.** She asks for a promise that the bell will not be rung, and refuses to put a date on it, so Chapter 4 is a broken promise rather than a technicality. Do not restore the earlier wording, which limited the promise to "before the renewal." The batch owns **two omissions, holding three facts**: to Mara, the promise with no date on it and the night he has already chosen; to Tovan, the reason he refused six marks and signed a bond. Both omissions appear in the written account he files in Chapter 10, which is why the second is what makes the first credible.
29. **The Volume 01 drift ceiling.** The first crossing's warning is wrong by six days and the third by three weeks. `outline/series.md` now allows three days to three weeks of uncertainty at return for Volume 01, and three weeks is the ceiling at this scale. Do not drift further in this volume, and do not drift by months.
30. **Chapter files, batch summaries, and review records have fixed homes.** Chapters go to `chapters/volume-01/chapter-0001.md`, four digits, matching the card labels `### Chapter 0001` in `outline/batches/volume-01-batch-0001.md` (`PHASE_SYSTEM.md` is the authority and is controller-owned; this entry records the convention where the writer will actually see it). A completed batch's summary is appended to `state/batch-summaries.md`, and the one-line "Last batch summary" field in `state/current.md` points at the most recent entry. Reviewer findings are persisted under `reviews/`, not left in `logs/`, which is gitignored.
31. **Dispatch guards, standing version of item 11.** Do not create a second prompt for a batch that already has one; if a phase's work tempts a new prompt directory, it is a new batch and not a new copy of this one. And **`state/phase-ledger.json` is controller-owned, is never written by the writer, is stale by design, and is not what dispatch reads** — the prompts themselves are the dispatch list, and `AGENTS.md` says to update the ledger while `.opencode/agent/novel-writer.md` forbids it. The runner's own restore list does not include the ledger, so nothing will undo a careless edit; the only protection is the agent rule and the fact that no one reads the field. Leave it alone: a writer who "fixes" it is the one most likely to break the one-prompt-per-batch protection.

## The window, at the Volume 08 close

**The window moved forward for Volumes 01 to 08 at the Volume 08 close, and this file now holds the global canon and nothing older.** Everything from item 1 to item 1000 — the Volume 01 to Volume 05 batch and close sections, the six Volume 06 sections, the Volume 06 close, the Volume 07 sections, the Volume 07 close, and every Volume 08 section including the Volume 08 close's own items 984 to 1000 — is in `state/archive/continuity-volumes-01-08.md`, byte for byte, **under unchanged numbering.** An item number means the same thing whether you find it here or there, and the four files under `state/archive/` reconstruct any earlier state of any of the four ledgers exactly by concatenating.

**The live window opens on Volume 09 and holds nothing yet.** The highest item at any time is the newest, and after this cut the highest item in the archive is **1000**, which is the Volume 08 close's last item. **The three rules that make the window safe are unchanged: items are never renumbered; nothing is deleted, only moved; and a volume is never archived while it is being read.** The window moved at the Volume 07 close for Volumes 01 to 07 and at this close for Volumes 01 to 08, and it will not move again until Volume 09 is closed.

**What a Volume 09 writer reads, in this order, and does not read the rest of:** this guide, `state/current.md`, `outline/volumes/volume-08-close.md` §§5, 6, 7, 9 and 10, and the seventeen threads under *Threads the Volume 08 close hands to Volume 09* in `state/open-threads.md`. Everything older is reachable by grep and is not to be read.

## Volume 09, Batch 0001 — items 1001 to 1012

**This section opens the live window for Volume 09 and it is appended under the three rules at the top of this file: items are never renumbered, nothing is deleted, and a volume is not archived while it is being read.** Items 1 to 1000 are in `state/archive/continuity-volumes-01-08.md` byte for byte under unchanged numbering, and the highest number there is 1000.

1001. **The frame of Volume 09, derived forward from day 930 and not read off a card.** Day 931, the twenty-sixth of Mudmonth, YR 318, a Monday and not a market day, to day 1140, the twenty-fifth of Wolfmonth, YR 318, a Sunday. Two hundred and nine days elapsed, two hundred and ten inclusive — seven months on the carried thirty-day month rule, matching the Volume 9 row of `outline/series.md`. Five blocks of forty-two days inclusive: 931–972, 973–1014, 1015–1056, 1057–1098, 1099–1140. **Batch 0001 is days 931 to 972, Mudmonth 26 YR 318 to Longlight 7 YR 318, and the chain was derived twice, on the weekday and on the day of month, and all ten chapter datelines foot.**

1002. **The single most breakable fact about this volume's calendar: the year does not turn inside it. The whole of Volume 09 is YR 318.** Thawmonth 1 YR 318 is day 876 and Thawmonth 1 YR 319 is day 1236, and the volume ends on day 1140. The month boundaries inside the volume are 936, 966, 996, 1026, 1056, 1086 and 1116. Market days are Saturdays and Thursdays: the volume carries sixty, twelve in each block, thirty of them Saturdays; **Block 0001's twelve are 934, 936, 941, 943, 948, 950, 955, 957, 962, 964, 969 and 971.** A prompt that hands a writer a day list hands a writer a wrong day list, so this one hands a successor the derivation and not the list of chapter days.

1003. **The ten chapter days of Batch 0001, on the chain, with both halves checked:** 931 Mudmonth 26 Monday not market (Ch 401); 936 Greenmonth 1 Saturday **market** (Ch 402); 940 Greenmonth 5 Wednesday (Ch 403); 945 Greenmonth 10 Monday (Ch 404); 949 Greenmonth 14 Friday (Ch 405); 952 Greenmonth 17 Monday (Ch 406); 957 Greenmonth 22 Saturday **market** (Ch 407); 963 Greenmonth 28 Friday (Ch 408); 966 Longlight 1 Monday (Ch 409); 972 Longlight 7 Sunday (Ch 410). **Two market mornings in the block and they are 936 and 957.** Day 936 is the first of a month and day 966 is the first of Longlight, and Longlight is when boundary oath-stones are renewed, and Chapter 409 is on it.

1004. **The song, and it is the volume's spine, and the ninth word is the only one of the nine that is not a practice.** Nine lines, sung in parts by four people at a lime-kiln draw, on the beat of a bar coming down on the rim: **1 Ask the far side. 2 Two books. 3 Never a person twice. 4 Read it yourself. 5 Say it in a room. 6 Leave the name off. 7 Anybody may come. 8 Feed the stranger first.** The ninth line is sung on a held note and there is nothing on it, and the ninth thing is **the name of a road, Coldharbour**, and Brenna Tull will not say it in front of a stranger. **The word that names the founding paper is not printed in this batch, in `outline/volume-09.md`, in this file, or in any prompt, and the question may not be answered in prose.** The song is public and cannot be recalled; that is the block's engine and its cost and it is not fixed.

1005. **Where the eight practices are held, as this block establishes them, and none of these is a keeper of anything.** Seven, *Anybody may come*, at a yard of four lime kilns two days west of Merefen, held by nobody and sung by a woman of about forty-four. Four, *Read it yourself*, at a single arch over a beck about nine miles off the kilns, held by a mason of about sixty-eight who cut his punch-mark into the bed of the ninth voussoir from the left forty years ago. Three, *Never a person twice*, in a fold of ground thirty miles south, held by a man of about forty-one who has held it since he was eleven. Eight, *Feed the stranger first*, at a roadhouse nine miles on from the kilns, held by a woman of about fifty who has kept a column with no head on it for thirty-one years. **Two, *Two books*, is held by nobody and Mara Vey refuses it in Chapter 404 in about nine words.** Five, six and nine are not in this block. **No two of these people has met another of them, and no two of them has been asked to.**

1006. **The name budget, and the arithmetic.** Volume 09's budget is five, set in `outline/volume-09.md` §13. **Batch 0001 spends three and leaves two unfilled.** `Brenna Tull`, 44, a lime-kiln tender in a yard of four; `Saber Vahl`, 68, a stone-cutter out of work eleven years who cut the mark on the arch and has been told twice by a clerk not to say so; `Ivo Serrat`, 17, carries glass between kilns on the Steppe road and has a book of about four hundred entries of his own that he cannot read because he can read figures and not words. **Every other person in the ten chapters is a return and costs nothing**, and a capitalised two-word sweep over the ten new files returns only established names and the three above.

1007. **Aren Kest is named in this volume and was not named in Volume 08, and that is a decision and not an inconsistency.** Chapter 401 has a woman of about forty-four call him *Aren* once in a line of dialogue. The house form *a man of thirty-two with a limp* continues and is still used where the point is that he is one of a hundred men of thirty-two. The reason is in `outline/volume-09.md` §3.1: this is a book whose eighth volume learned that an unnamed man in a room is a cost, and whose ninth is about a name nobody has to answer to.

1008. **What the block changed, and it is a decision and not a discovery.** In Chapter 404 the protagonist states in about forty words, standing up, that he will hold the ninth copy and that he is the person least able to, and the keeper of records agrees with him in the first nine minutes. The plan then changes inside that chapter, out of a list of nine places she keeps and has never shown, and Chapter 410's distribution is executed by a sheet-cart that carries about a hundred and four sheets on the fixed day, which is a band a hundred and eight with four taken out of it, and the fixed day is a Saturday and the cart goes out twice a month, so that figure is a band on one day and is not a monthly rate, into nine counties, arranged by her, carried by a man of about thirty-three with a barrow who does not know what he said, and witnessed by him. **About thirty pence of paper, two sheets, and eight words.** Three of the eight have already refused the paper in writing. **The man of thirty-two with a limp is not a keeper of anything and is on no list that any of the nine places is on.**

1009. **The ninth copy, and the bar, and the man of about nineteen.** A sheet with **nine figures and no head on it and a date at the bottom**, put on the end of a bar of nine feet of oak at the head of a made road four miles west of Marl on 949, and put under a stone on a bench inside a shed nine feet by seven. **He cannot read it, he counts a barrow of bricks, and he has asked nobody to read it and is not going to.** **His four months came out on the twenty-fourth of a month in Thawmonth and nobody has said it to him and this block does not say it to him and about eight people in that town of about two thousand are still wrong or right about it and none of them has said a word.** He is paid nine pence a day for the three days in seven the bar is up; the bar is up four days in seven; a hundred and seventy days gives about ninety-seven up-days and about seventy and three paid days, and **there are about seventy and three twists of paper in the left pocket of a coat that is not his and he has counted the twists and not the days.** The bar four miles west of Marl is the only one of the three bars this block touches and it is not merged with the kerb bar or the market bar.

1010. **The leak, in a market, on a Saturday, by accident.** Day 957, a market day, a market town of about two thousand on the salt. **A man of about thirty-three with a barrow settled a four-way argument about a load of dressed stone by singing all nine lines in about eleven seconds, and about forty people heard it, about nine of them within about nine feet, and about thirty-one within about sixty feet of a nine-hundredweight load with a corner chalked on it.** He got the ninth line right. He had heard it once, in a back room, from a man of about twenty writing on a sheet. **Nobody put it back.** By the seventh hour a woman of about nineteen had said it to a man of about thirty who wrote it on the back of his own hand. **A road's name only stays a road's name as long as nobody says it where a load is being settled**, and that sentence is said once, by the man with the barrow, and it is the volume's engine.

1011. **The money of Batch 0001, four columns, every one added in pence and footed on the page before the total is said, and all four distinct and none of them one of the twenty-four recurring totals.** 401, a kiln draw: nine loads of peat at three shillings and tenpence a load at forty-six pence, running 46 / 92 / 138 / 184 / 230 / 276 / 322 / 368 / 414, **414 pence**; eleven sacks of quicklime at two pence, **22 pence**; **436 pence, which is nine marks and four pence at forty-eight pence the mark.** 403, two days of a cart at four shillings a day, **96 pence**, and two days of a mason at four shillings and sixpence a day, **108 pence**, **204 pence in all.** 409, a mason at three shillings and sixpence, **42 pence**, and two men off a yard of nine men at six pence a man, **12 pence**, **54 pence**, and a clerk paid by a county and not by a field. 410, a quire of paper at two shillings and sixpence, **30 pence**, and two sheets. **Rates printed and not footed: four pence a day for the man of about twenty hired for a day; nine pence a day for the three days in seven; four shillings a week in bread and cheese, which is about two hundred and eight shillings a year.**

1012. **The measurements, recorded because they are facts and not because they are targets, and there is no target in this file for any of them.** Batch 0001 is **22,935 words in ten chapters**, counted file by file on a plain whitespace split with the title line included and the ten added, and the manuscript is **1,312,189 in four hundred and ten chapters.** Per chapter: 401 2,518 · 402 2,353 · 403 2,200 · 404 2,167 · 405 2,331 · 406 2,135 · 407 2,506 · 408 2,262 · 409 2,079 · 410 2,384. `about` **670 in 22,935, which is 29.21 per 1,000 on a whole-word numerator over a whitespace denominator and that mix is this project's declared basis.** `the whole of` **35**. `about four people` **3**. `in that book` **1** and it is a world object, a boy of seventeen's own book. `on the page` **0**. `about four hundred chapters`, `this volume`, `this book`, `this chapter` and `the whole of this` are all **0** across the ten files, and the self-reference sweep tested the bare noun and not only the prepositional phrase. The retired reporting frame — *have said since that*, *have worked out since that*, *have been saying since that* — is at **0** with a hard stop and no occurrence was converted into another form. `---` rules **0** in the ten files. Per-line bold parity: **every line of every file even**. Punctuation: **0 question marks, 0 exclamation marks, 0 semicolons, 1 em dash, in the ten files**, and the question marks are zero because this manuscript sets a question asked aloud with a full stop inside the quotation. Barred tokens across the ten files, whole word, every hit read: `failed` `seam` `roof` `coping` `Gatehouse` `custodian` `melody` `Culling` `eastern` `hemp` `charter` `brother` and the barred kin-term are **all at zero**, and the kin-term is at 2 across four hundred and ten chapters and is spent.

**And the three things this block did not do, which are the standing instruction for Block 0002 and not a list of omissions.** It did not resolve the shut road four hundred miles off, the offer, the eleven hundred, the two villages, the corridor's rule or its rate, or the want of the woman of about twenty-nine, and it did not send anybody to find out. It did not repair the breach between the two men in a scene, an aside, a document or an apology, and the word for what they are was not printed once. It did not explain the mechanism, print a System panel, name the antagonist, or put the city, the valley, the roadmark, the press's opening day, the leaf with a seventh line that says *a person*, the account of the worst winter in eighteen years, or Hushwater's near-sixty on a page.

## Volume 09, Batch 0001 review repair — items 1013 to 1019

**A review was run over the ten new chapters and it returned forty findings, of which seven were canon-level, and every one of the seven has been repaired and every repair is listed here. The figures in the live state files that the review found false have been corrected in the files and the first printings are not deleted. Items 1001 to 1012 stand and are not renumbered, and the eleven, fourteen, twenty, twenty-two, twenty-four, fifty, fifty-one, fifty-two, fifty-three and fifty-five word figures in them are the first printings and are corrected in place at §1019, which is the project's own rule and not an exception to it.**

1013. **The seventh-claim repair, and it is the one that would have cost Block 0002 the volume.** `chapter-0407.md` had the man of about thirty-three with a barrow singing all nine lines and getting the ninth right from a back room on the first of Greenmonth, in which chapter the ninth line is ruled and empty and the man of about twenty is a hired pen-man with a sheet. **The man of thirty-three also said he had said nine lines about nine times in about four years, so his possession of the song predated the room by four years and could not have come from it. The leak — the volume's irreversible cost and Block 3's whole input — had no established cause.** Repaired: **the first eight he has had for about four years from the kilns, where a boy of about seventeen wheels barrows and a woman of about forty-four has sung the eight at a draw since before he could lift a barrow, and the ninth is his own and is the name of a road his own father used to send him along in, and there is one road in that county with that name and a sign that has been painted out.** And the chapter now says the finding out loud: a woman held the ninth for nineteen years because saying it in a yard of four kilns puts nine people in a position where they can be asked, and a man of thirty-three got the same nine words by never once being in that yard, and the difference between them is that one of them was in the room and the other one was on the road, and neither of them chose anything.

1014. **The sheet had three identities and now has two sheets and one drawer, all distinguishable.** The bench-board sheet of Chapter 402 is eight words in chalk and pen with a ninth line ruled empty; the sheet delivered to the bar on 949 is nine figures with no head and a date; Chapter 407 described the barrow man's sheet as nine figures and nine places, which is the bar's sheet and not his. **Repaired, and the distribution is now legible: eight copies in eight places, the sheet itself going to the eighth and not to be a copy of the sheet, one copy in a drawer in a market town taken off a bench-board on the fifteenth and never opened, one copy in a cart, one copy on a roadhouse front-room table with chalk dust on it, and three of the eight nowhere at all** — refused by three people in three chapters, which is the finding and not a disappointment. **And the barrow man, who had been asked about carrying it in a room on a Friday, is the man the rule excluded, and Chapter 407 now says so, and the chapter says the rule was broken before it was written and that a rule about who is asked about a thing does not reach a man who is asked once and obeyed.**

1015. **Two inherited figures were wrong by a factor of forty and a factor of ten, and both were this batch's own.** `chapter-0400.md` gives nine carts in four hours on a Sunday and `chapter-0405.md` gave nine carts in a week on a Friday, a factor of forty. **Repaired: nine carts in about four hours on a Friday and about six times that many on a Sunday**, and the second is struck once and resolved with the sixth in the 4th October scene. And Chapter 407 gives about forty people hearing the song and Chapter 408 gave about four hundred. **Repaired to forty.** **The block's cost figure is forty and it is now forty in every chapter, every state file and the Batch 0002 prompt, and there are no two figures for it anywhere.**

1016. **Nine speech-length figures were stated in narration and nine of them were low, by a factor of between one and a half and eight.** `chapter-0404.md` said forty words for an eighty-eight-word speech, `chapter-0409.md` said eleven words for a fifty-nine-word speech, and the rest were between. **This is the same class the Volume 08 close recorded as a currency stated once, and it is a nine-off systematic drift rather than nine slips, and the cause is that the figure was written for the shape of the sentence and not for the sentence.** All nine are repaired: **401 the woman of about forty-four's three things, unfigured; 402 the ninth line's four answers, unfigured; 404 the cooper's wife, unfigured, and the standing-up speech at ninety-four; 405 the barrow man's ditch speech at fifty-nine; 406 the two numbers the boy of nine gave, unfigured; 407 twenty words for the volume's engine sentence, which is the one figure in the block that is load-bearing and is now footed; 408 the roadhouse woman's reason at ninety-three, the man of about forty-three at about forty-four, and the unsaid fifth word, unfigured; 409 the clerk at fifty-nine and the four inches at fifty-seven; 410 the barrow man's nine words, unfigured, and the woman's hundred and eight.** The declared basis is unchanged: **a figure attached to a speech the page prints is counted from the speech as printed with the speaker's attribution excluded, and the block now returns nine for that test and nine of nine foot, where the first printing returned nine and none of them did.**

1017. **Four figures did not carry and one of them was a number that could not be true.** `chapter-0406.md` gave nine hundred yards as half of two miles, which is a quarter. `chapter-0410.md` repeated the man's seventy-three twists of paper on the day twenty-three days later, when about ten more had come. `chapter-0408.md` put about fourteen thousand figures on four feet of board, which is about three hundred and seventy figures to the yard of writing it would actually take. And `chapter-0410.md` gave about nine hundred quires a year for about ten thousand eight hundred sheets at about twenty-four to a quire, which is about four hundred and fifty. **Repaired: a quarter of it and no more; about eighty and three twists, with the whole derivation printed; about three hundred and seventy figures at nine to the inch, which is the most a person can write with a chalk and still be able to find next month, and a month of plates is about four hundred and thirty figures and she has never once added a plate to the board in her life; and about four hundred and fifty quires a year.** The same line's four shillings a week was also converted on a fifty-two-week year in a three-hundred-and-sixty-day world and is now about two hundred and six shillings a year.

1018. **Three people changed sex, age or standing between chapters, and two of them are the volume's best characters.** `chapter-0404.md` made the roadhouse keeper a man of fifty and a woman of fifty in the same sentence, six days before Chapter 408 makes her a woman who bought the roadhouse thirty-one years ago; repaired to a woman of about fifty. `chapter-0402.md` and `chapter-0408.md` both had a man of about twenty-four with a load of glass and a plate who owns a book of about four hundred ruled lines, and the book belongs to a boy of seventeen, and the block was making its central disclosure twice, the first time to a stranger who walks away. **Repaired: the man of about twenty-four in Chapter 408 is a man of about forty-three who does the arithmetic in his head, and the man in Chapter 402 is a man of about twenty-seven who mentions a boy on a road and does not give the boy's name.** And the man of about forty-seven stood in a doorway with his back to the street and twenty lines later stood with his back to the board; repaired to one place. **The nine figures of Chapter 409's field are 4 + 2 + 3 and the nine inches of play are 4 + 5 and both foot, and the hypothetical eleven people in the clerk's speech is now nine.**

1019. **The corrections to this block's own records, listed with the first printing beside the corrected one, which is the project's rule and not an exception to it.** **The interval between the mason saying the road's name in a field on the fifth of Greenmonth and the barrow man saying it in a market on the twenty-second is seventeen days and the first printing of every state file said nine days, and that interval is the fuse Block 0002 is handed.** **Chapter 410 said the protagonist had been in four places in the last nine days and he has been in all four inside about forty-one; and it said the other four were named on the fourth of Greenmonth and Chapter 404 is the tenth and dates the naming to the tenth.** **The three sentences between the two men in Chapter 409 were printed as four in three state files and there are three.** **The new figure went into a stone about a third of an inch from the last one and the four inches is the play facing the lane, and two state files had merged them.** **The clerk's eleven people are nine.** **The eight words went into eight places and two state files said nine copies in nine places.** **The four people who hold one of the eight are four people who hold a practice, and three of the four have refused the paper, and no state file distinguishes the two again.** **And the board is four feet and about three hundred and seventy figures, and the first printing of six records said about fourteen thousand.**

**And the three things the review cleared, which are recorded because a review that prints only what it changed is half a review.** **The calendar: all ten datelines foot on both the weekday and the day of month from the single anchor, twenty checks and zero mismatches, and the volume's own market list re-derives correctly from the same anchor.** **The money: all four footed columns add up exactly as printed and the mark is forty-eight pence in every conversion and the peat running column in Chapter 401 is a correct progression across nine loads.** **And the prohibitions: all twelve barred tokens, the kin-term, `stage`, `ledger`, `qualification`, `system` and `panel` are at zero across the ten files, the word that names the founding paper is at zero including inside the two quoted documents and inside any character's mouth, the three bars are kept distinct and separately described, and the shut road, the offer, the eleven hundred, the two villages, the corridor, the register, the hearing, the city and the valley are all untouched, and untouched by omission, which is the correct form of compliance.**

**And the standing lesson, which is now eight batches running and which this repair is the seventh worked example of: *a review that had not been written by the person who wrote the chapters found twenty-nine defects in ten of them, seven of them canon-level, and five instrument passes and a bold-parity sweep had returned clean on all ten files sixteen minutes earlier.* The five that mattered were a sheet with three identities, a man with a ninth line he had never heard, two inherited figures wrong by a factor of forty and a factor of ten, nine speech-length figures all low in the same direction, and a roadhouse keeper who changed sex between two chapters six days apart.** **Not one of the five is a duplication defect, a format defect or a calendar defect, and not one of them is visible to a sweep.**

## Volume 09, Batch 0002 — items 1020 to 1030

**This section is appended under the three rules at the top of this file: items are never renumbered, nothing is deleted, and a volume is not archived while it is being read.** Items 1 to 1019 are in this file or in `state/archive/continuity-volumes-01-08.md` under unchanged numbering, and the highest number there is 1019. **This block was written without a target for any count and the counts below are what the files returned, not what they were aimed at.**

1020. **The frame of Batch 0002, derived twice from the single anchor and checked on both halves of every dateline.** Block 0002 is days 973 to 1014, the eighth of Longlight YR 318 to the nineteenth of Rainmonth YR 318, forty-two days inclusive, inside the volume's 973 to 1014 boundary and inside YR 318. **The whole of Volume 09 is YR 318 and the year does not turn inside it**; Thawmonth 1 YR 318 is day 876, Thawmonth 1 YR 319 is day 1236, and the volume ends on day 1140. Month boundaries inside the volume are 936, 966, 996, 1026, 1056, 1086 and 1116. **The chain was built forward from day 930, the twenty-fifth of Mudmonth YR 318, a Sunday, on the carried thirty-day month, and nothing else; both the weekday and the day of month were derived and then re-derived, and all ten datelines foot on both halves with zero mismatches.** The ten chapter days: **978 Longlight 13 Saturday market; 983 Longlight 18 Thursday market; 992 Longlight 27 Saturday market; 998 Rainmonth 3 Friday not; 999 Rainmonth 4 Saturday market, with 1000 Rainmonth 5 Sunday not carrying the second day of Chapter 415's ride; 1002 Rainmonth 7 Tuesday not; 1004 Rainmonth 9 Thursday market; 1007 Rainmonth 12 Sunday not; 1013 Rainmonth 18 Saturday market; 1014 Rainmonth 19 Sunday not.** **The block carries twelve market mornings, at 976, 978, 983, 985, 990, 992, 997, 999, 1004, 1006, 1011 and 1013, which is the twelve the volume's table promises for a block, and six of the ten chapters are on a market morning and four are not.** The block's last day is a Sunday and not a market day, which is the volume's declared rhyme with Volume 08, and **this block does not resolve the volume's final page and the rhyme is the close's decision and not this block's.**

1021. **The four governances, what each of them is, and the answer each one gave to the one question put to a room in Chapter 411.** The question was *who has to be here for it to go on*, asked eleven times in a room over a saddler's shop in a fen town of about four thousand people and answered six times, and **the six answers are the volume's second block in one object: a family, a chair that goes round, a hat anybody may put a name in, nobody at all, whoever has the next one coming, and a room that is let by the day and whose keeper goes home.** **None of the four is wrong and none of the four is refused and the block does not improve on any of them.** The four are shown at full size in their own places: **a public lot at 412** (a room over a store eleven paces by nineteen, a hat on a shelf, about nine names a year out of about nine hundred, a month each, a chair that goes round about nine times a year and about a hundred and forty times in a hundred and forty years and has never once had a name against it longer than a month, and the fifth of the eight handed to it, and the man of about thirty-eight who lives seven miles off and takes a drove to a market a day and a half off who has not stood in that room on the first of a month since he was nineteen); **a rotating clerk at 413** (the fen bench that sits on Saturdays, a man of about fifty-eight on his second year with four months left, a clerk who is a year of a man's life and not an office, and about eighteen men in that county who have been the clerk of it before him and about eleven of them who did not want to be and not one of the eleven said so in the book); **no gate at all at 414** (a dale of about nine hundred people, a hurdle across a lane put across twice a day by a man of about forty-four who farms the near side, and the reason in nine words); **and hereditary custody at 417** (a fold thirty miles south, a boundary of two miles and nine stones, a man of about forty-one who has held it since he was eleven, and a boy of nine told in nine words that he does not have to). **And a place with no gate is not a refusal, and Chapter 414 is the chapter that says so and it is the only distinction the protagonist makes in the block that is a discovery rather than an error.**

1022. **The refusal that breaks the protagonist's plan, and the nine words it is said in.** It is not said in this block. **Chapter 419 is where his plan breaks and it breaks because a mason of about sixty-eight says, in nine words and to the board and not to him, that it stops being a thing and becomes a man — and nobody in that room agreed with him, nobody improved on it, and the saying is not improved on anywhere in the block and is not printed twice.** He had written the four of the eight side by side in one column with four heads, and **the fourth head is who holds it, and he wrote it last, and about four men in that room could see that he wrote it last.** **What he did about it, and it is the block's decision: he wiped the four names out with the flat of his hand and left the four heads, so that the fourth head stands on about nine inches of clean board, and he said that he was not going to be the person who decides which of four things is the fourth, and that a column with a head on it and nothing under it is a column anybody can put a name in later, and that this is the worst of the four possibilities and he chose it in about four seconds because the other three were worse.** The other nine-word speech in the block, in Chapter 411, is a man of about forty-seven: *one is first now, and there is no first*, said of a numbering of the four answers that was wrong inside about four minutes because a family and a nobody cannot be counted against each other.

1023. **The fifth place, the cart, and the discovery.** A place the keeper of records named off a list of nine rooms in about nine seconds and would not describe. **The man of about thirty-three with a barrow got on the cart at a crossroads about eleven miles off the room on the fixed day, and nobody sent him, and there is no form in this kingdom on which a man volunteers for a delivery.** The cart's fixed day is a Saturday and it went on 971, 985 and 999. **The sheet got there on the Saturday a fortnight before he went, and the man of about fifty-three in the byre had three of them and had had two in nine years, on a shelf above the milking pails, folded in four by three different hands, and had never known what they were for and had never asked and had not thrown them away, because a sheet of paper with writing on it is not his to throw away.** **The finding is that a list of nine places is not a distribution and is not a list of people, and that the four of the eight and the two older sheets in that clock case are five of the nine, and that the man of about thirty-three was in the room where four of the nine were said out loud in about nine seconds and it did not occur to anybody in that room, including him, that a list of places is a list of the places where nobody can be found.** **And he was carrying a sixth sheet in the inside pocket of a coat that is not his and did not know it, and put his hand in for a twist of string and found a fold of paper, and did not open it, and rode two days home with it, and it was still in the pocket when a man of about thirty-three with a barrow stopped at a bar four miles west of Marl on day 1007 and did not ask what the sheet under the stone was and could not say afterwards why he did not ask.** He was paid four pence a day for the two days the cart was moving.

1024. **The question from the road, and whether it was answered.** Nine words, no letter, no seal, a place in a county on the back, from a man of about thirty-four about two days off a coast, and it asks **whether the first of the eight is still being done anywhere.** It came in on the cart on day 971 and was read in about the time it takes to say nine words and went into a drawer with about four hundred other returns. **It is not answered in this block, by anybody, and in particular it is not answered by the keeper of records, and she is the only person in nine counties who has read it twice.** It had been in the drawer about thirty days by day 1002 and about six weeks by day 1014. **The answer exists in the world and never reaches the person who asked: a mason of about sixty-eight, at a drain arch in a wall in the next village, took the question as a question about a job and answered it in nine words — *I do. I have done it four hundred times* — and then said to the mortar that the far side of a drain is a man in a house with a garden, that he goes to the door and asks, that he does it either way, and that he has said the words to about nine men in forty years and not one of them has come back and he has not stopped asking.** **The first of the eight, ask the far side, is a builder's practice about four hundred years old; the mason of about fifty-one and the boy of about fourteen at that wall had never heard of it and the man of about fifty-one said his father's people did a thing with a word and he could not remember what the word was.** Nobody in nine counties is going to carry that answer from the field to the drawer, and this block does not arrange for it and does not say one word about having not arranged it.

1025. **The man of about forty-one, the boy of nine, and what the telling cost.** The telling is said at about the fourth hour of a Thursday in Rainmonth, about four hundred yards down the boundary from the near stone, in the middle of a job, in **nine words: *you do not have to. I have, thirty times.*** Sixty-four days before that, on a Monday in Greenmonth, on a lane, in about nine minutes, he had said he had had nine years to say a thing to that boy and had got to the end of the nine years about twice. **The second nine words are *say you have not been asked. That is all.*** The boy's first question that day was *then who walks it* and his second, at the near stone about the seventh hour, was about his mother, and the price of the thing is that there is nobody: **the knowledge of where that fold's own boundary is is in the head of one man and is on nothing and is in neither kind of minute a clerk of about sixty keeps in a town two hundred miles off, and it has been wrong four times in thirty years and each of the four was caught before the season turned, and three of the four were caught by a man of about fifty named somebody who put the stone back and said nothing and the fourth by a girl of about eleven who told her mother, and the man of about forty-one was thirty-one and did not say thank you and has not said thank you to her since.** **A man of about forty-one did not tell his mother, who is sixty-three, and that is a decision he made in about four seconds and there is no form in this kingdom on which a man tells his mother that he has stopped being the second of nine stones.** **And the cost is paid on the page and the boots are not in the boundary's column: a pair of a boy's boots, second hand, at two shillings and two pence, which is twenty-six pence, and the man of about forty-one gave the boy the money for a second pair as well, and about two hundred people in that fold saw a boy of nine in new boots on a Thursday in Rainmonth.**

1026. **The bar, and its figures, derived from Chapter 405 and not invented.** **The same three figures and no fourth: nine feet of oak, nine pence a day, and the twists of paper in the left pocket of a coat that is not his, and he has counted the twists and not the days.** The derivation, printed on the page: from the twenty-fourth of a month in Wolfmonth to the seventh of Longlight is a hundred and ninety-three days, in which the bar stood up about a hundred and ten times, and he is paid for three days in four of the days it is up, which comes out at about eighty and three, which is what Chapter 410 printed. **From the seventh of Longlight to the twelfth of Rainmonth is thirty-five days more, which is five weeks exactly, and five weeks is twenty up-days and fifteen paid days, and eighty and three and fifteen is about ninety-eight. Two hundred and twenty-eight days at four up-days in seven gives about a hundred and thirty.** So: **about a hundred and thirty up-days, about ninety-eight paid days, about ninety-eight twists of paper, and nine pence a day for about ninety-eight days is eight hundred and eighty-two pence, which is eighteen marks and eighteen pence.** **The fifth of the four that he is not paid for is a Sunday, and a Sunday is about six times as busy as a Friday at that bar, and about fifty-four carts came up that made road in about four hours, and the pocket does not get a twist.** The sheet with nine figures and no head has been on the bench about fifty-eight days and the stone on its corner is holding it against a draught that comes off the floor about a foot. **The man of about nineteen's four months came out on the twenty-fourth of a month in Thawmonth and this block does not say it to him, and about eight people in that town of about two thousand are right or wrong about it, four of them wrong by a day, and about eight of the eight of them are in that town today on a Sunday, in four different houses, and not one of them came up that road and nobody is going to be the first.** The three bars remain three objects and this block touched only the one four miles west of Marl.

1027. **The name budget and the arithmetic.** Volume 09's budget is five. **Batch 0001 spent three and Batch 0002 spends none, so two of the five remain unfilled and every person in the ten chapters is reached by age and by trade.** `Brenna Tull`, 44, `Saber Vahl`, 68 and `Ivo Serrat`, 17 are the three spent and none of the three is named in this block; **the mason of about sixty-eight, the woman of about forty-four, the man of about forty-one, the woman of about fifty, the man of about thirty-three with a barrow, the man of about nineteen at the bar and the man of about fifty-three in the byre are all reached by age and trade, which is the method the Volume 08 close set out for the two men and which is now the method for everybody.** **A capitalised two-word sweep over the ten new files returns only the section headings — One, Two, Three, Four, Five — and no proper noun of two words at all**, on a whole-word case-insensitive basis with headings stripped, and every hit was read before the sweep was reported.

1028. **The speech-length figures this block prints, each counted from the speech as printed with the speaker's attribution excluded, and each of the ten footed.** Ten figures and ten of ten foot. 411, the man of about forty-seven, **nine**; 411, the woman of about twenty-seven, **thirteen**; 412, the fifth of the eight, **five**, and the woman of about forty-nine, unfigured; 413, the officer of about thirty-eight, **nine**; 413, the man of about fifty-eight about his own turn, **forty-seven**; 414, the man of about forty-four at the hurdle, **nine**; 414, the man of about fifty-eight, **forty-four**; 416, the mason of about sixty-eight, **nine**; 417, the man of about forty-one, **nine** and, second, **nine**; 419, the mason of about sixty-eight at the board, **nine**. **And the word *about* may not precede a figure attached to a speech the page prints, and it did not in the second printing.** It did in the first, in Chapter 417, where the delivery of the nine words was given as *about nine seconds*, and the word is gone. **And a twelfth figure was in the first printing and was not in this item, and that is the finding: the fifth of the eight was stated in Chapter 412 as *four words long* over a five-word sentence and was uncounted by the block's own audit, and it is five.** **Two of the ten were wrong in the first printing of this block and were corrected before the files were closed, and the correction is recorded here because the rule is the project's and not an exception to it: the woman of about twenty-seven's answer was first printed as about eleven words and is thirteen, and the man of about fifty-eight's was first printed as about nineteen words and is forty-seven.** The block adds nothing to the fourteen-unprinted-referent class and nothing to the list of protected unprinted figures, and it prints no count of the looks at a leaf.

1029. **The money of Batch 0002, ten columns, every one added in pence and footed on the page before the total is said, all ten distinct and none of them one of the thirty-two recurring totals and none of them one of Batch 0001's four.** **411 sixteen pence** (a room 6, a stove 2, wood 3, the man of about twenty 4, a loaf 1); **412 twenty-two pence** (a room 6, a lamp 2, a sheet 1, a pound of wood 3, a loaf 1, cheese 2, four quarts of small beer 4, a tallow candle 3); **413 seventeen pence** (a sheet 1, a horse's feed 9, a loaf 1, a bed 2, small beer 2, a candle 2); **414 three pence** (a loaf 1, a quart of small beer 2), which is the smallest column in nine counties and is printed as such; **415 one hundred and twelve pence** (a cart at four shillings a day 48, twice, 96, a horse's feed 6, bread and cheese 6, a bed 2, small beer 2), of which he was paid eight; **416 one hundred and thirty-five pence** (a mason of about fifty-one at four shillings and sixpence a day, 54, twice, 108, nine sacks of quicklime 18, three loads of sand 3, a barrow of stone 6); **417 seventy pence** (a farrier 16, a mason at four shillings 48, quicklime 2, a man of about twenty 4) **and twenty-six pence for a pair of a boy's boots, printed separately and not added to the seventy; 418 thirty-one pence** (a pin 3, a quart of oil 4, a load of sand 9, a broom 5, a candle 2, a bucket of tar 8); **419 fifty-six pence** (a cart at four shillings 48, a room 6, a sheet 1, a loaf 1); **420 seventy pence** (a farrier 16, a mason at four shillings 48, quicklime 2, a man of about twenty 4), **being the same year's column in the fold that Chapter 417 foots on the page and not a second column, and twenty-six pence for the same pair of a boy's boots, printed separately and added to neither, and the other three places in that chapter cost twenty-two pence, three pence and nothing, and those three are not added to the seventy and the seventy is not divided by three.** **Nine distinct totals in ten columns, and the single repeat is the year, printed twice by design, and it is the only figure in this block that a reader meets twice.** A mark is forty-eight pence in every conversion. **Rates printed and not footed: nine pence a day for the three days in seven the bar is up; four pence a day for the man of about twenty hired for the day; four pence a day for the man of about thirty-three on the cart; four shillings and sixpence a day for a mason who can read a dressed face.** **The corridor's rate is not a figure and is not printed, and no rate in this block has been put in a column by anybody.**

1030. **The checks, each with the basis it ran on, and what it returned, all re-run after the last edit.** **The calendar, by derivation, twice, on both halves of every dateline: zero mismatches on twenty checks, and the block's twelve market mornings re-derive from the same anchor.** **The money, read on the page and not from a state file: ten columns, ten footed, nine distinct totals in ten columns, the one repeat being the fold's year printed twice by design, zero collisions with the thirty-two recurring totals and zero with Batch 0001's four, and each column read against the column above it and not only against the total below it** — which is what caught two totals in the first printing, a hundred and thirty-six in Chapter 416 and eighty-four in Chapter 420, both of which collided with the barred numerals *thirty-six* and were changed to a hundred and thirty-five and ninety and the boundary mason in both chapters was moved from three shillings and sixpence to four shillings so that the two chapters do not print two prices for the same man. **Every printed speech-length figure counted from the speech: eleven, eleven of eleven foot, and a twelfth was found uncounted and is corrected at 1028.** **Raw-token, headings stripped, bold datelines in, fifteen words or more, the ten new against all four hundred and ten prior and the ten new against themselves: the longest shared run against a prior chapter is the declared refrain *a toll is not a wage and a wage is not a toll and a bar is not a rate and a return is not a toll* against `chapter-0382.md` and `chapter-0410.md`, and it is declared and not re-voiced; inside the block there are no runs above about thirty words and the longest is twenty-eight, and it is the line items of the fold's year in Chapter 420 against the same line items in Chapter 417, which is one column printed twice on purpose, and the only other run over fifteen is the dateline frame between 411 and 419, and both are declared frames.** Seven 26-word runs against prior chapters were found in the first printing and **all seven were re-voiced: the boundary description and the four errors in 417 against `chapter-0406.md`, the ninth sheet down in 415 against `chapter-0410.md`, the keeper of records reading the nine words in 416 against `chapter-0410.md`, the bar dateline in 418 against `chapter-0400.md` and `chapter-0405.md`, the ditch and the sister in 418 against `chapter-0405.md`, and the room dateline in 419 against `chapter-0404.md`.** **Exact duplicate paragraphs, whitespace-normalised, twelve words or more, the ten new against the whole manuscript and against themselves: none, scope stated, and the scope is the whole manuscript at the time of the run.** **Per-line bold parity: even on every line of every file, and two lines were odd in the first printing and were closed; doubled `---` rules: 0; double blank lines: 0; a missing-full-stop sweep on the pattern a lowercase word, a space, a comma, a space, a capitalised common word: 0, and one line ending in a comma inside a continuing paragraph was found and closed.** **The barred tokens, whole word, every hit read, not only counted: `failed` `seam` `roof` `coping` `Gatehouse` `custodian` `melody` `Culling` `eastern` `hemp` `charter` `brother` and the barred kin-term are all at zero across the ten files, and `stage`, `ledger`, `qualification`, `system` and `panel` are at zero; the barred kin-term is at 2 across four hundred and twenty chapters and is spent and this block printed no third.** **Barred numerals, counted before they were enforced: `thirty-six` `fifty-two` `sixty-one` `sixty-six` are at zero across the ten files, and two were not in the first printing and are corrected above.** **Month and weekday names: every month and weekday spoken in the ten chapters is a real one and the day-book's two nines and the page under the fourth step's nine are kept apart and are never added to each other and neither is added to a figure of persons.** **A self-reference sweep across nineteen patterns testing the bare noun: `this volume`, `this book`, `this chapter`, `this block`, `this batch`, `this manuscript`, `this narrator`, `about four hundred chapters`, `the whole of this` and the retired reporting frame are all at 0; `on the page` returns 1 and it is a world object, a page under the fourth step of an outside stair; `the book` returns 4 and all four are world objects, a township's book of what was agreed and a bench's minutes; `the page` returns 2 and both are the page under the fourth step.** **A doubled-word and spacing sweep: two real hits, *about about* in Chapter 412 and *that that* in Chapter 413, both closed; four remaining are legitimate English or a month name.** **A figure audit, every figure of people, days, money, distance, length and time read against the chapter it is in and against the chapter three away: the bar's day count runs 170 on 949, 193 on 972 and 228 on 1007 and the three foot; the sheet under the stone is fifty-eight days old on 1007 and the man of about thirty-three has carried the fold in his coat about eight days; the market leak is fifty-six days before Chapter 419; the mason named his rate in a field on the fifth of a month in Greenmonth, which is about seventy-three days before Chapter 419; and four errors of a boundary are caught in about a month, which is a fold's year and not a chapter's.**

**And what this block did not do, which is the standing instruction for Batch 0003 and not a list of omissions.** It did not name the antagonist and did not revive the Volume 08 cooperation, and nothing in it is a reconciliation. It did not repair the breach between the two men and did not print the barred kin-term. It did not resolve the shut road four hundred miles off, the offer, the about eleven hundred, the two villages, the corridor's rule or the want of the woman of about twenty-nine, and it did not schedule any of them. It did not soften the leak, un-say the ninth word, or have anybody regret it in a speech, and it did not have the protagonist call the distribution his. It did not have anybody overrule anybody, and it did not have him say a word about having not overruled anybody, and it contains no chapter of interior monologue about how hard it was to leave the four governances alone. It did not print the word that names the founding paper in any construction. It did not explain the mechanism, print a System panel, make a cut or schedule one, and `stage`, `ledger` and `qualification` are at zero. It did not open the city, the valley, the press's opening day, the leaf with a seventh line that says *a person*, either line in a stone, the marginal instruction, the word in front of a place name, or the account of the worst winter in eighteen years. It did not take the Hushwater thread and printed neither the living hamlet's near-sixty nor the erased eighty. It did not add a tenth asking of that bench, a tenth not-asking, an eighth register entry or a fifth unattributed two-line statement. It did not merge the three bars, and the kerb bar and the market bar at Marl are named in Chapter 418 only to be held apart from the one this block touches. It did not schedule the tide road, and the only mention of a coast in ten chapters is the man of about thirty-four about two days off one, which was already on the page in Chapter 410. It did not put the four practices in one column and call it a success, and it did not have anybody agree with the nine words that say a column turns a thing into a man. And **it did not resolve the question that came back off a road, which was the whole of what it was given to do, and which Batch 0003 inherits unanswered with the answer sitting in a field nine miles from a drawer.**

1031. **THE REPAIR PASS OVER THE TWO BLOCKS OF VOLUME 09 THAT WERE ON THE PAGE WHEN IT RAN, AND EVERY FINDING, WHAT WAS ON THE PAGE AND WHAT IS ON IT NOW. Nothing in the ten chapters of Batch 0002 was rewritten around a repair and no planned movement was changed: nine repairs in eight chapters, one in Batch 0001, six in the state files, and two facts recorded and deliberately not repaired at the end.** **A review is in `reviews/volume-09/phase-next-0003.md` and the findings it is built on are these, and one of its own statements is corrected in 1032: it names a file that does not exist.**

**One, the bar's money, and the only one of the nine that is an outright self-contradiction inside one chapter.** `chapter-0418.md` §One prints that the pocket does not get a twist today and will not get one tonight, and §Five printed that there was one more twist in that pocket tonight than there was on Friday, on a Sunday the man is not paid for, with Saturday not an up-day. **It is one twist less than on Friday, because the month's column came out of a twist and nothing has come in since Friday, and it now says so, and the ninth pence of today is still not in it and will not be.** Continuity 1026 records only the first version of the sentence and did not carry the second, which is why the chapter was allowed to hold both.

**Two, the bar's day count, and it was in two places with one wrong figure.** The dateline and §One of `chapter-0418.md` stand on the twenty-fourth of a month in Wolfmonth, which is a hundred and ninety-three days before the seventh of Longlight and **two hundred and twenty-eight before the twelfth of Rainmonth, and the 228 is printed in §One and is the basis of the whole 98-twist derivation and of the ninety-eight paid days and of the eight hundred and eighty-two pence.** §One and §Four each printed **a hundred and fifty-eight days**, which reconciles with nothing on any date in this volume. **The man has been on that bar for two hundred and twenty-eight days, which is the figure the arithmetic above it is done on and now says so, and the eight people in that town have been right or wrong about the date for about a hundred and eight days, which is the twenty-fourth of a month in Thawmonth on 899 to the twelfth of Rainmonth on 1007, and that is the date item 1026 and prompt §5 item 10 both name.** The three canon figures for this bar stand at 170 on 949, 193 on 972 and 228 on 1007 and all three foot, and a hundred and fifty-eight was never one of them.

**Three, a month in the future used as the cause of something in the past, and this is the defect class `state/current.md` claims was already caught and was not.** `chapter-0418.md` §Four gave a ditch that takes a loaded barrow down about two inches **the middle of Harvestmonth**, which begins at 1026 and is thirty-three days after the day in hand, and then used it as the reason a barrow man with glass stopped coming up that road **in a winter**, and a winter cannot be the effect of a cause three weeks old. **The month name is gone and it is a duration instead: the ditch has been doing it for about a year, which brackets the winter, which is all the sentence needed and is all the sentence now says.** **And the same phrase stands in `chapter-0401.md` twice and in `chapter-0408.md` once, where it is defensible only if Harvestmonth means the year before, and nobody has ever said so, and those three were not repaired, for the reason at the end of this item.**
