**Four, the office's own output, and it was arithmetically impossible.** `chapter-0413.md` printed about four hundred sheets a month going out of that office into nine counties, and then printed about nine hundred of them carrying a place, a figure and a trade, which is more than the four hundred and which destroys the two nines the paragraph exists to set side by side. **Canon is the room's own about nine hundred sheets a month into nine counties, and the eight words went out in a band of about a hundred and four on the fixed day, and the two are two different series.** Chapter 413 now prints **about nine hundred a month, of which about nine have nine words on them and the other about eight hundred and ninety have a place and a figure and a trade on them**, which foots, both figures carrying *about* on the page, as nine and about eight hundred and ninety in about nine hundred, and it keeps the difference between the two nines where the chapter puts it.

**Five, the closed-road distance in a place where it is not the closed road.** `chapter-0419.md` put the market where about forty people heard the ninth line about four hundred miles from a room with a door on a lane, and that room is two hundred miles off in 415, 416, 420 and this prompt, and four hundred is the protected distance of the shut road and the city. **It is two hundred miles now**, and the fifty-six days is untouched and is correct, 1013 less 957.

**Six, forty miles attached to the arch instead of to the mason, twice.** `chapter-0416.md` §Five and `chapter-0419.md` §Five both said the rate of four shillings and sixpence a day was named in a field at a beck nine miles off the kilns and then added **and about forty miles off**, which reads as the arch being forty miles off. **Forty miles is where the mason is, not where the arch is, and Chapter 403 is the chapter that says so.** Both now read **at an arch nine miles off the kilns ... and he is a man who is about forty miles off that arch**, and the seventy-three days in 419 is untouched and is correct, 1013 less 940.

**Seven, a public lot that does not add up.** Nine names a year at a month each covers nine months of twelve, and `chapter-0412.md` closed on **a month in every nine with nobody in that chair at all**, which is neither nine months filled nor three months empty. **It is three months in every twelve with nobody in that chair at all, and nobody in that township can tell you which three they will be.** The same wrong figure was in `state/current.md`, `state/chapter-summaries.md` and `state/open-threads.md` and is corrected in all three.

**Eight, an unanchored weekday in the block's last chapter.** `chapter-0420.md` said a thing was taken on **on the Thursday**, and the nearest Thursday to the nineteenth of Rainmonth is the sixteenth, three days earlier, and the room took it on the eighteenth of Longlight, twenty-eight days earlier, so the bare word resolved to a different week entirely. **It is the eighteenth of Longlight, which was a Thursday**, which is Chapter 412's own dateline and needs no derivation.

**Nine, the sheet in two places at once.** `chapter-0418.md` §Two has the sheet on the bench under the stone in the shed nine feet by seven and §Four had the man who put it there having put it **on the end of that bar**, with no word saying it had come off. Both are true and the chapter did not say so. **§Four now says the sheet is not on that bar and is under the stone on that bench**, and the fifty-eight days the sheet has been on the bench are untouched.

**Ten, in Batch 0001, and it is the one this item exists mostly for.** `state/chapter-summaries.md` records as a repaired defect of Chapter 404 that **the cart carries about a hundred and four sheets and not about four hundred**, and `chapter-0404.md` line 91 was corrected to a hundred and four and **line 13 still said about four hundred sheets in a banded bundle, three hundred sheets from the corrected figure in the same chapter about nine lines away.** It is a hundred and four now, and it is the two words that put Batch 0001 at 22,937, and both are footed in `state/batch-summaries.md` rather than absorbed.

**Eleven, the two figures of the room, and they were both true and were not reconcilable as written.** `state/continuity.md` 1008 said the sheet-cart carries **about a hundred and four sheets a month**, and this prompt's §6 said **about nine hundred sheets a month into nine counties, two rounds a year**, and neither footed against the other because 1008 is a band on one day and §6 is a different series going the other way. **The resolution, now in 1008, in this prompt's §6 and in `outline/volume-09.md` §5.4, is that about a hundred and four is one band on the fixed day, a band a hundred and eight with four taken out of it, the fixed day is a Saturday, the cart goes out twice a month and is back a fortnight later, and about nine hundred a month is the room's own other series. The two are counts of two different things, neither is derived from the other, and no chapter may add them or convert one into the other.** Chapter 420 §Three printed a hundred and four as a monthly rate and is corrected for the same reason, and `outline/volume-09.md` §5.4 said two rounds a year where the round is twice a month.

**Twelve, the two counts of the block's own money, and both were wrong in the same direction.** `state/current.md` and this prompt's §4 both claimed **eleven** footed money columns and both listed twenty-six pence among them, and Chapter 417 prints that the boots are not in its column and Chapter 420 prints them inside the ninety as a line item, so there are **ten footed columns at 16, 22, 17, 3, 112, 135, 70, 31, 56 and 90 pence, and twenty-six is a line item inside two of them and is added to neither.** Continuity 1029 had it right and said ten and the other three files did not. And five files said **the thirty-three recurring totals** where the list enumerated in this prompt's §4 holds **thirty-two**, which was counted and is now thirty-two in all five.

**The two facts recorded and not repaired, and why.** **The middle of Harvestmonth in Chapters 401 and 408**: a repair pass is not the place to re-derive a calendar, the two volumes' chains are already known to disagree by a day at a year turn, and the phrase is defensible there on the reading that a month name means its most recent occurrence before the day in hand. **So the rule is written down where the next writer will meet it, in this prompt's §7 item 1, and a writer who cannot resolve a month name to a day is told to change the sentence into a duration, which is what Chapter 418 now does.** **And the review record**: there is no review file for `volume-08/batch-0002` through `0005` or for any Volume 09 batch, and the last on the page was `reviews/volume-07/batch-0005.md`, so the gate in `AGENTS.md` was unmet for seven batches. **This phase writes `reviews/volume-09/phase-next-0003.md` and repairs nothing it cannot see, because a review file written about chapters nobody re-read is a review file about nothing.**

**And the two flags that are not this phase's to clear.** `workspace/continuation/next-0003/.wip-conflict` is a stale runner-owned marker on a phase that went on to complete and commit Chapters 411 to 420, and **`state/phase-ledger.json` still reads `phase-000-bootstrap` and `planned` with `attempts: 0`** after the whole of Volume 08 and two blocks of Volume 09. Both are recorded in `state/current.md` under the live flags, both were in the repository before this phase began, and **a writer does not create, retire or mark a prompt it did not write and does not write the controller's ledger.**

**The measurements after the repair, recounted on the declared basis and not carried over.** Batch 0002 is **19,760 in ten chapters** and the manuscript is **1,331,951 in four hundred and twenty chapters**, and **Batch 0002's `about` is 613 in 19,760, which is 31.02 per 1,000**, on a whole-word numerator over a whitespace denominator. Re-run on the ten files after the last edit: question marks, exclamation marks, semicolons and em dashes **0, 0, 0, 0**; `---` rules **0**; per-line bold parity even on every line of every file; the twelve barred tokens and `stage` `ledger` `qualification` `system` `panel` at **0**; the barred numerals `thirty-six` `fifty-two` `sixty-one` `sixty-six` at **0**; `the whole of` **29** and `about four people` **4**, both unchanged by the repair. **No prose was added or cut anywhere except the two words in Chapter 404 and the repairs named above, and every figure in the six state files that these repairs moved was recounted and rewritten, because a state file that reports a number nobody re-counted is the one thing in this project that has gone stale nine times.**

1032. **THE SECOND REPAIR OVER BATCH 0002, WHICH RAN AFTER `reviews/volume-09/phase-next-0003.md` HAD ALREADY BEEN WRITTEN, AND EVERY FINDING WITH THE ARITHMETIC. No chapter was restarted, no planned movement was changed, no name, no dateline, no ending type and no canon fact was added or removed, and the repair is in eight chapters of Batch 0002, one figure in Chapter 405, and six records. Nineteen defects, nineteen real. The first repair's own count of twelve was not wrong about what it found; it was wrong about being finished, and the difference between the two passes is the finding worth keeping.**

1033. **The four that are contradictions inside a single chapter, and these are the class a re-read of one file catches and a comparison of two does not.** **411 changed the sex of the woman who let the room** — a man of about twenty-seven asked the question *herself* and gave the sixth answer, and sixteen lines later the same person is a woman who said *leave them*. **412 both struck a name out of the hat and did not** — the woman of about sixty-three took the man's name out with a piece of chalk, put a line through it and wrote the other eight in, and Chapter 420 builds its whole township paragraph on the half that denies it. **The hat was not struck.** **417 said the boy of nine asked one question and it was the only one he asked that day, and then had him ask a second one about his mother at the near stone; he asked two, and the first of the two is now printed as the first of two.** And **420 had the day-book open in front of the keeper of records and unopened in the next clause of the same sentence.** Each of the four is a fact a reader meets as a chapter and not as a record.

1034. **Four arithmetic failures, and three of them are the class the block's own §7 item 9 was written to catch.** **412 called the fifth of the eight *four words long* over the five-word sentence *say it in a room*, and no instrument saw it because the block's own audit did not know the figure was there.** **413 gave a nineteen-year-old rotation of one-year terms and about four men in that county who had held the post, which is four against nineteen, and is now eighteen before the present man and about eleven who did not want to be.** **412 said the chair had gone round about twenty times in a hundred and forty years, and nine names a year at a month each is about nine times a year and about a hundred and forty in a hundred and forty years.** And **414 footed a two-item column as *one, two, three is three pence*, and a nine-mile lane as nine miles when the chapter says nine miles out and nine miles back.**

1035. **Three dates that could not be true, and all three were an interval that was not derived.** **417 put a conversation on a Friday in Greenmonth, and Chapter 406 puts it on a Monday, the seventeenth.** **417 called the walk of the first of Longlight *nine days ago*, and the chapter is day 1004 and the first of Longlight is day 966, which is thirty-eight.** **417 called the same Greenmonth lane conversation *nine days ago* as well, and it is sixty-four.** And **418 had a man of about thirty-three with a barrow say the ditch was not his, against Chapter 405's own speech in which he says *I came up here three times last winter and I did not come a fourth* and Chapter 408's *he is the reason the man of about thirty-three with a barrow stopped coming up that road in the winter*. Two chapters name him and the third invented a second man to hold the fact. The two threads are now one man**, standing nine feet off the bar with the fold of paper still in the coat.

1036. **Two figures that were correct in Chapter 405 and stale in Chapter 418, and one of them is the stale-duration class exactly.** **The bundle behind the stove was about a hundred and twenty days old on day 949 and is about a hundred and seventy-eight days old on day 1007, and Chapter 418 lifted the paragraph across with the old figure on it, verbatim, thirty words and more.** And **the bar's kerb was printed as about nine hundred yards of kerb in a chapter whose own dateline says nine feet of oak across nine feet of a kerb**, a feet-and-yards slip of about a hundred and sixty times. The same sentence gave a ditch at about nine hundred yards west of the shed where Chapters 405 and 408 both say four hundred, and **Chapter 405 line 81 said nine hundred against its own line 35, so that figure was corrected in Batch 0001 for the same reason Batch 0001's Chapter 404 was: two chapters and a third against it, and no record claimed it was clean.**

1037. **The money column of the fold, which two chapters footed two ways and which a state file had recorded as resolved while the page still said the opposite.** **Chapter 417 foots the year at seventy pence, sixteen and forty-eight and two and four, and prints the twenty-six pence of a pair of a boy's boots separately and outside it. Chapter 420 footed the same year at ninety pence by putting the boots in and dropping the quicklime and the man of about twenty.** The year is one column. **It is now seventy pence in both, with the same four line items and the boots printed separately and added to neither**, which leaves **nine distinct totals in ten columns, and the single repeat is the year printed twice on purpose**, and the block's own claim of ten distinct totals is corrected at 1029. **The other three places in Chapter 420 now cost twenty-two pence, three pence and nothing**, which are the township, the dale and the room and are derivable from Chapters 412, 414 and the absence of any column in the room, and the price of a cart is gone from that sentence because a cart is Chapter 415's cost and Chapter 415 is a byre and not one of the four.

1038. **Three defects of the narrator's own footing sentence, which item 1030 had declared a frame, and a frame that is also a defect cannot be declared.** **411's column of five items has one wage in it and the chapter said two, and 416's column of four has one wage and said two, and the sentence was the same in both and wrong in both.** It is now *there is one line in it that is a wage and four that are not* and *one line in it that is a wage and three that are not*, and the word *line* makes it exact. **Item 1030's declaration of that sentence as a frame is withdrawn in 1032 and the run it was declared against no longer exists**, because the two chapters no longer share it. The fourth was **416 saying he is spoken by one man about four times a year to about four men, in a chapter that says he has said the words to about nine men in forty years and has done it four hundred times** — which is ten times a year, and the chapter now says ten and nine, and *about four hundred men have said yes* became *a man on the other side has said yes every time of it*, because four hundred men and nine men cannot both be true.

1039. **Six defects a single sentence of grammar or reference would stop a reader on, and this is the class that no count of anything finds.** **417's last section had a subordinate clause with no main clause**, *and a man who has answered a thing that plainly is not asked it again by a stranger in a field*, run into by a comma, and **the same sentence said the nine seconds happened in a lane the protagonist was not in, when Chapter 406 has him met in that lane.** **419 wrote that he wrote the four heads and then wrote *them*,** so the heads were written twice. **419 told the reader the decision *is printed here because it is the decision*,** which is the narrator's own artefact and the only self-reference any instrument missed, because the sweep tested the noun and not the concept. **420 described the sheet on a bench under a stone as *a hole in a wall*.** **418 printed a clause that does not parse**, *and a Sunday is a day that parish goes to the next parish*. And **420 said the protagonist was fed at two doors in a dale where Chapter 414 has one plate**, and it is one door.

1040. **Three figures that belonged to one object and were attached to another, and these are the class the previous review had already found once in this very block.** **419 gave the mason's cart two distances in one section**, thirty miles out and thirty back at the chapter's head and nine miles out at the foot of the same column, and the cart is out of a yard about nine miles off. **420 imported the four inches of a field oath-stone in another county into the fold's boundary thirty miles south**, with a clerk of about sixty who has nothing to do with those nine stones and whom the chapter then says does not know he is wrong, which the Chapter 409 clerk does, and **it denied that the fold has ever argued about its boundary, against three caught errors in thirty years in Chapter 417.** The four inches is now the foot to the left at the four hundredth yard, which is the fold's own fact. And **420's list of four things a person can point at in the rain held three governances and a sheet of paper under a stone**, so **the fourth is the hurdle across the dale lane**, and all four are now the four the block is about.

1041. **And what the nineteen were not, which is recorded because a repair pass that reports only what it changed is half a repair.** **The ten datelines foot on both halves, derived twice from the day-930 anchor, on weekday and on day of month, and the twelve market mornings re-derive from the same anchor. The bar's arithmetic is the best-derived material in the block and is untouched: 193 days from day 779, which is the twenty-fourth of a month in Wolfmonth of the year before, and Wolfmonth is correct in the dateline and was checked against a year of three hundred and sixty days and nine named months rather than against the assumption that the year turns. Nine pence for about ninety-eight days is eight hundred and eighty-two pence, which is eighteen marks and eighteen pence. All ten money columns foot in pence. All twelve barred tokens, the four barred numerals, `stage`, `ledger`, `qualification`, `system` and `panel` are at zero. Zero question marks, exclamation marks, semicolons and em dashes. Zero rules, zero odd bold lines, zero triple newlines, zero space-after-comma, zero space-before-stop, zero missing full stops, zero retired-frame hits, zero self-references. Zero exact duplicate paragraphs of twelve words or more inside the block and none anywhere in four hundred and twenty files that involves any of the ten, and the thirty-two that the sweep returns are all in Volumes 01 to 07 and are documents and frames. The longest shared run against four hundred and ten prior chapters is thirty-two words and it is the declared refrain. The name budget is three of five spent and this block spent none, and a capitalised two-word sweep over the ten files returns zero proper nouns of two words at all.**

1042. **THE THIRD PASS OVER BATCH 0002, WHICH RAN WHEN THE REVIEW PHASE'S OWN LOG TURNED OUT TO HOLD NO FINDINGS, AND EVERY FINDING WITH THE ARITHMETIC. No chapter was restarted, no planned movement was changed, no name, no dateline, no ending type and no canon fact was added or removed, and the repair is in five chapters of Batch 0002 and in five records. Five defects, five real. The block is 19,774 and the manuscript 1,331,965.** **The review of this batch ran and returned its findings only to `logs/batch-0002.review.log`, which is gitignored, and it was cut off by a rejected tool call while it was measuring its own sweeps, so it never wrote a findings list and never applied anything. `logs/batch-0002.review.log` is a transcript and not a list: it re-verified every mechanical claim the block's own records make, it stopped on the one figure it could not settle, and it stopped there. This pass is the findings list that review owed, and `reviews/volume-09/batch-0002.md` is the durable copy of it.**

1043. **The one open measurement the review could not settle was a method error and not a count, and the state file was right.** The log counted the token with a command that splits on the space and not on the newline, and returned **612**, against the **613** in `state/current.md` and `state/open-threads.md`. **A token that begins a line is glued to the token that ended the line before it, and the count is an undercount that no declared basis produces.** The project's basis is a whitespace token with punctuation stripped from its ends, counted case-insensitively, and on that basis the ten files return **613 before this pass and 614 after it**, and `the whole of` returns **29** and `about four people` returns **4**, both of which the log confirmed. **No figure moved because of the log.** What the log did establish is that the block's own recorded measurements all reproduce exactly, which is worth one line in a file nobody had written down: the block total 19,760, the manuscript 1,331,951 in four hundred and twenty files, all twelve barred tokens at zero, `stage` `ledger` `qualification` `system` `panel` at zero, the four barred numerals at zero, zero question marks, exclamation marks, semicolons and em dashes, zero `---` rules, and the per-file counts 2,035 - 2,073 - 2,003 - 1,747 - 1,877 - 2,009 - 2,056 - 2,052 - 1,956 - 1,952. **Every one of them reproduced to the digit on the declared basis.**

1044. **The four that are contradictions between two chapters, and one of them is a defect the second pass had already found in the other chapter and rationalised instead of repairing.** **411 contradicted 412 about the township chair on both of the things the chair is.** 411 had a man of about fifty-eight say that the chair goes round, that **a man sits in it a year**, and that it **has never once had a name in it**; 412 has nine names a year at a month each, the chair going round **about nine times a year and about a hundred and forty times in a hundred and forty years**, anybody may put a name in the hat, and the chair has never once had anybody's name against it **for longer than a month**. **Nine names a year at a month each is nine months filled and three months empty, and a term of a year is a year the chair does not have.** The second pass found this and did not repair it: `state/batch-summaries.md` recorded it as a chair term of a year and of a month in two chapters that are about two different chairs, and the two are not two chairs. **The chair and the hat are in the same township of about nine hundred people in both chapters, 411's third answer describes the hat in that township and 412 describes the room it stands in, and 411's own line that a chair and a hat cannot be counted against each other needs them as two items and does not need them to be two chairs.** The two clauses are now **a man sits in it about a month and then it goes to the next** and **nobody in that township can tell you from one month to the next who is in it**, which is the same answer to who has to be here and is true in 412. **The lesson is the eleventh batch running of it and it is the plainest statement this project has made: a repair pass that explains a defect away has not repaired it, and the explanation goes into a state file where the next writer reads it as a decision.**
1045. **Three more, and each is a figure or a place attached to the wrong object, which is the class item 1040 names.** **413 gave its own town two populations**: the dateline is **a fen town of about four thousand people** and §Three's last clause is **the Crown's own series in a town of about two thousand**, in a chapter whose subject is that town and whose preceding clause is *about two hundred people in that town*. **About two thousand on the salt is the protected figure of Marl and it is Chapter 418's and Chapter 419's figure, so the collision is with a place this block holds elsewhere and not with a passing number.** It is **that town of about four thousand** now. **414 put the forty-four words at a gate**, in the one chapter in the block whose finding is that **a place with no gate does not say no**, and the sentence is a deliberate echo of the nine words said standing at the hurdle and of **the two stops that were not at a gate** in §Two; and the speaker came **out of the second house**, so the words were said at a door and not at a hurdle either. It is **outside the second house** now. **415 kept the clause the second pass orphaned**: the second pass made the man of about thirty-three with a barrow one man in Chapters 405, 408, 415 and 418, and 418 now says **the quarter of a mile round that shoulder is the reason the man of about thirty-three with a barrow stopped coming up that road in the winter**, against 405's own speech in which he says *I came up here three times last winter and I did not come a fourth*. **415 still said the ditch *was not his ditch***, which is the wording of the pre-repair version in which a second man held the fact, and the barrow man is the one it is about. It is **is not on this road** now, which is what the sentence was for.

1046. **The records, and the two of them are the class this project has paid for most often and both are about a repair rather than about a chapter.** **`state/current.md` still carried the fourth of the four things a person can point at in the rain as *a hole in a wall with nine figures in it under a stone*** — the exact phrase item 1039 records as a defect found and repaired in Chapter 0420, where the fourth is the hurdle across the dale lane. The repair was applied to the chapter and not to the handoff, and the handoff is the file a writer reads first.** And `state/chapter-summaries.md`'s ten block summaries and its list of the ten endings were written before both repairs and contradicted the pages on fifteen counts**: a fifth line struck out and not struck out, the fifth of the eight at four words, a chair empty once in every nine, a name struck out of a hat, about four men in a nineteen-year rotation, five or six of the nine, two days home, a boy's only question, the eight people in the next parish, a cart that went thirty miles, four places about eleven miles apart, the room over a door on a lane, and about four inches of one of nine stones. **All fifteen are now the page's figures and not the pre-repair ones, and the two chapters that were summaries of repairs are left standing as they were, because a record of what a repair changed is worth more than a record of the latest number.**

1047. **And what the five were not, on the same basis as item 1041, because a repair pass that reports only what it changed is half a repair.** **The ten datelines still foot on both halves against the day-930 anchor and the twelve market mornings still re-derive from it. The bar's arithmetic is untouched and still foots: 193 days from day 779, about a hundred and ten up-days, about eighty and three twists, thirty-five days more being five weeks exactly, twenty up-days and fifteen paid days, ninety-eight twists, 882 pence, eighteen marks and eighteen pence. All ten money columns foot in pence, nine distinct totals in ten columns with the one repeat being the fold's own year printed twice on purpose, and the boots at twenty-six pence outside the seventy in both chapters. All twelve speech-length figures foot, recounted speech by speech from the printed words, and none is preceded by *about*. The longest shared run against all four hundred and ten prior chapters is the declared refrain and it is thirty-three words and not the thirty-two the second pass recorded, the extra word being the trailing *and there is not a* that follows the refrain in Chapter 420 and is part of the same run on a maximal basis.** The instrument matters twice over and both halves are worth writing down: **a sliding-window measurement reports one hit per word of a run rather than one run, and returns 157 for a block that has four**, so the run has to be collapsed to its maximal extent before it is counted, and the second pass's thirty-two is the same run measured one word short of its end. **The finding the figure exists to support is unchanged, which is the other half of the note: the only reuse across the block and four hundred and ten chapters above fifteen words is the declared refrain.** Zero exact duplicate paragraphs of twelve words or more inside the block. The twelve barred tokens, the four barred numerals, `stage` `ledger` `qualification` `system` `panel` at zero. Zero question marks, exclamation marks, semicolons, em dashes and `---` rules. Zero odd bold lines, zero triple newlines, zero space after a comma, zero space before a stop. Zero self-reference across the nineteen patterns testing the bare noun. **The name budget is three of five spent and this pass spent none, and a capitalised two-word sweep over the ten files returns zero proper nouns of two words. The volume's own calendar was re-derived from the boundaries at 876, 906, 936, 966, 996, 1026, 1056, 1086, 1116 and 1146, and Thawmonth 24 is day 899, which is the date Chapter 418's hundred and eight days requires, and the twenty-fourth of a month in Wolfmonth of the year before is day 779, which is the bar's 193.** **And one figure in the plan was measured and deliberately not repaired: `outline/volume-09.md`'s calendar table gives 965, the thirtieth of Greenmonth, as a Wednesday. It is a Sunday, and 966, the first of Longlight, is a Monday in the same table, and the two cannot both be true. No chapter depends on it, the ten datelines foot without it, and a repair pass does not edit the volume's authority on a one-word weekday that no page reads. It is recorded here for whoever owns the outline, and the prompt's §7 rule — a month name that cannot be resolved to a day becomes a duration — does not cover a weekday, and the next writer who touches that table should derive the row rather than copy it.**

---

## Volume 09, Batch 0003 — Chapters 421 to 430, "What a Name Costs When It Is Sung"

**This section is written by the phase that wrote Batch 0003 and it is Batch 0004's whole instruction. `outline/volume-09.md` is the plan the block was written against and is binding. Every figure was counted on the chapter files as they stand and the basis is named. Nothing here renumbers anything above it and nothing here resolves anything Batch 0001, Batch 0002 or this block left open.** Twelve items, and the twelfth is what the successor must not do.

1048. **The frame, derived twice from the single anchor, on both halves of every dateline, and the fact that five of the ten chapter days are in Harvestmonth and the block's own prompt did not say so.** Day 930, the twenty-fifth of Mudmonth YR 318, a Sunday, and the carried thirty-day month, with month boundaries at 876, 906, 936, 966, 996, 1026, 1056, 1086 and 1116. **Chapter days: 1016, 1017, 1019, 1020, 1029, 1031, 1033, 1034, 1038 and 1056, that is the twenty-first, twenty-second, twenty-fourth and twenty-fifth of Rainmonth, a Tuesday, a Wednesday, a Friday and a Saturday, the fourth, sixth, eighth, ninth and thirteenth of Harvestmonth, a Monday, a Wednesday, a Friday, a Saturday and a Wednesday, and the first of Fallowmonth, a Sunday, and the four days of Chapter 428 are the ninth, tenth, eleventh and twelfth of Harvestmonth, a Saturday, a Sunday, a Monday and a Tuesday.** **Two derivations were run and they did not agree with each other, and that is the finding: the day-of-month half footed on all twenty checks in the first printing, and the weekday half did not, and seven labels in four chapters were wrong, and the second pass repaired the months, declared the block clean, and certified both halves. The weekday defect is item 1049 defect ten and its repair is item 1063.** **The year does not turn inside Volume 09 and the whole of it is YR 318, and Thawmonth 1 YR 319 is day 1236.** The block's twelve market mornings are 1018, 1020, 1025, 1027, 1032, 1034, 1039, 1041, 1046, 1048, 1053 and 1055, re-derived and not read off a card. **Two of the ten chapters fall on a market morning and eight do not, and the two are Chapter 424 on the twenty-fifth of Rainmonth and Chapter 428 on its first of the four days. The first printing of this item said five and five, and it is wrong, and the reason it is wrong is that it was counted off the weekday labels and the weekday labels are what was wrong.** **And the single most breakable fact about this block's calendar is that Harvestmonth begins at day 1026 and every chapter from 1029 onward is in Harvestmonth, and the count at the stile begins on the fourth hour of 1018 in a dateline that says the twenty-third of Rainmonth and is right, and 1026 is the first of Harvestmonth and a Friday.** A writer who reads a chapter number as a day of month will get every one of the five Harvestmonth datelines wrong, and that is exactly what happened in the first printing of this block and is item 1049, **and a writer who reads a day of month as a weekday will get the other five wrong in the other direction, and that is the mirror of the same error and it is what this block did.**

1049. **Ten defects the block found in its own first printing by hand, and repaired, and the eleventh was not found by the block at all, and the class is the class this project has paid for most.** **One, the month, twice over and in five chapters.** The block's first printing carried 1029, 1031, 1033, 1034, 1035, 1036, 1037 and 1038 as days of Rainmonth, and Rainmonth is 996 to 1025, so every one of them is a day of Harvestmonth, and 1020 was printed as the twentieth of Rainmonth when it is the twenty-fifth, and 1017 as the seventeenth when it is the twenty-second, and 1019 as the nineteenth when it is the twenty-fourth, and 1016 as the sixteenth when it is the twenty-first. **The derivation had been run on the day number instead of on the day of month, which is the fifth running of that exact error in this project and the error the prompt's §2 warns about in a sentence.** **Two, an authorial note left in a chapter file.** A working note beginning *Hmm — careful* stood in `chapter-0429.md` between two sections and was caught by a sweep for stray notes and not by any instrument the project owns. **Three, a money column that did not foot.** Chapter 423 printed nine line items, a nine-number sum, and a total of twenty-nine, and the nine numbers are twenty-five. A barrow hired for the day at four pence was added and the sum list was not. **Four, a printed speech figure that was wrong by two words.** `chapter-0427.md` printed *the nine words* for *I have got that mark nine times*, which is seven words, and the chapter printed the figure twice. **Five, the word *about* before a figure attached to a speech the page prints**, which the prompt's §5 item 12 forbids, in Chapter 428, where *about ten words to say* stood against a speech the chapter prints. **Six, four self-references.** *This chapter* twice and *about four hundred and twenty chapters* once, and the last is the bare-noun form the prompt's §7 item 7 requires to be tested. **Seven, a figure in a speech claim that belongs to no speech on the page.** Chapter 424 printed *a sentence of forty-four words* about a morning in another room, and no speech of forty-four words is printed in Chapter 413. **Eight, a bar count.** The block's own prompt said Chapter 429 is *about a hundred and thirty days later than the last time this was on a page*, and a hundred and thirty is Chapter 418's own **up-day** count, not an interval, and 429 is thirty-one days after Chapter 418. **Nine, two internal contradictions in a single chapter.** Chapter 424 printed the third line as written at the third hour in its first section and at the seventh hour in its fourth, and printed the form as complete the day before the day it goes out on and seven days before it. **And a tenth, found by the duplication pass and not by reading: nine runs of twenty-eight words or more against prior chapters, all of them re-voicings, now re-voiced, item by item, in the two paragraphs below.**

**And an eleventh, not found by the block, not found by any of the block's own sweeps, and found by a person reading the datelines against the chain on a later pass: seven weekday labels in four chapters were wrong, and every day-of-month figure in all ten was right.** Chapter 425 was printed *a Saturday and a market day* and is a Monday and is not a market day. Chapter 426 was printed *a Monday* and is a Wednesday. Chapter 427 was printed *a Wednesday* and is a Friday. Chapter 428's four days were printed *a Thursday, a Friday, a Saturday and a Sunday* and are a Saturday, a Sunday, a Monday and a Tuesday, and it printed *two of the four are market days* over one. **All seven are reproduced exactly, seven of seven and with no false positives, by anchoring the day *of month* on Rainmonth 1 at day 996 instead of Harvestmonth 1 at day 1026: 1029 becomes 999 a Saturday, 1031 becomes 1001 a Monday, 1033 becomes 1003 a Wednesday and 1034 to 1037 become 1004 to 1007 a Thursday to a Sunday. Chapters 421 to 424, 429 and 430 were derived from the day *number* and came out right, and that is why six of ten passed.** **This is the mirror image of defect one in the same block and it is the same mistake: defect one derived the month from the chapter number and defect eleven derived the weekday from the day of month, and the block's own prompt §2 named only the first direction, so the check that was run twice was validating the half that was already right.** **It is also the second running in this project of a class that a review cannot see by comparing two files, because every one of the seven labels is internally consistent with its own chapter: the wrong weekday was in the dateline, in the body of the chapter, and in the state files that certify it, and the certification was what made it survive.** The repair, and what it cost in prose, is item 1063.

1050. **The three invented things, with the day each was entered and the person who entered it, and the whole of the damage is that nobody compared them.** The form is a county form with **three heads cut about forty years ago in a room two counties off by a man who is not alive: a place, a figure and a trade.** **A place, `Coldharbour`, entered on the twenty-second of Rainmonth, day 1017, a Wednesday, by the man of about thirty-four with a sheet, from a man of about forty-four who farms about forty acres and has a ditch at the bottom of it where a boat used to come up, and who said, in seventeen words, `It was a harbour once and it is my field now and both of those are true.`** The field is about two hundred yards off a made road, the lane off that road crosses the field, goes to a stile and then goes on half a mile and joins the nine-mile road. **A figure of persons, `four hundred and seventy`, entered on the twenty-fourth of Rainmonth, day 1019, a Friday, by the same man, from a count of about a day and a half at that stile by about nine people, the four hundred and seventy being footed on the page as fifty-eight, forty-four, thirty-nine, seventy-one, thirty-three, sixty-two, forty-seven, sixty and fifty-six.** The count is honest and the thing counted is not what anybody thinks it is: about nine tenths of the four hundred and seventy were on the nine-mile road and not in the field, and there are about thirty-four people in that field and in that lane. **A trade, `salt carting`, entered on the twenty-fifth of Rainmonth, day 1020, a Saturday and a market day, by the same man, from two carriers he asked in a yard in Merefen in about nine minutes.** **The three were entered on three different days, in three different places, by three different sets of people, and the form was finished on the twenty-fifth and went out on the next fixed Saturday, day 1027, in the middle of a band of about a hundred and four sheets, and it has been in four counties since, and by the first of Fallowmonth it is being said in about nine houses in four counties by people who are not looking at a form.** **And there is no villain to catch and no chapter in this block corrected any of the three and no chapter in this block says so out loud.**

1051. **The man of about thirty-three with a barrow's second asking, and what he was asked.** He was asked **in a yard in a market town of about two thousand people on the salt on the fourth of Harvestmonth, day 1029, a Monday and not a market day**, by **a man of about fifty-three who takes loads up a made road and is not an officer and is on nobody's strength**, who had read the three off a copy a clerk of about twenty-three read out in a room over a saddler's about nine miles off, and who told him **a place with a figure on it gets carts put on it, and that is how it is done.** He said he did not know what a figure on a place got a man, and the man of about fifty-three said that neither did he and that it did not matter. **In Chapter 429, on the thirteenth of Harvestmonth, day 1038, he asked the man of about nineteen at the bar four miles west of Marl what is on the sheet on that bench, which is the wrong question, and the boy said `I do not know and I cannot read and nobody has ever asked me either` — fifteen words — and was believed, and the boy did not ask him back what is at the end of the road, which is the question he could have answered and the boy could not.** The fold in the inside pocket of a coat that is not his is **thirty-eight days old at 1038 and thirty-seven at 1036 and has not been opened**, and there are now **two** pieces of paper in that county with about nine figures on them and no head and a date at the bottom, and not one person in four counties can read either of them. **The third asking, on the Monday that is the third of the four days of Chapter 428, day 1036, was the boy asking the man, and the man did not read it and did not say he would not.**

1052. **The question from a road, and whether it was answered, and it was not.** Nine words, no letter, no seal, a place in the county on the back, from a man of about thirty-four about two days off a coast, asking **whether the first of the eight is still being done anywhere.** In a drawer with about four hundred other returns since the seventh of Longlight, about eleven weeks by the first of Fallowmonth. **She has not answered it and this block does not have her answer it, and nobody in nine counties is going to.** The answer is still where it was: a mason of about sixty-eight has asked the far side about four hundred times, the last time of all on the seventh of Rainmonth at a drain arch in a wall in the next village, in nine words, to a stranger who had asked him a question about a job. **Nobody carried it from the field to the drawer in this block and the protagonist went to the field in Chapter 426 and did not carry anything back, and he did not write the answer down and the notebook is eleven lines on a page with no words on any of them.**

1053. **The bar four miles west of Marl, and its figures derived from Chapters 405, 410 and 418 and not invented.** Nine feet of oak, up four days in seven on a Monday, a Wednesday, a Friday and a Sunday, down on the other three, and the man of about nineteen is paid **nine pence a day for three of the four up-days, and the one he is not paid for is the Sunday.** **The start of it is day 779, the twenty-fourth of a month in Wolfmonth of the year before, and the three canon figures are a hundred and seventy days on 949, a hundred and ninety-three on 972 and two hundred and twenty-eight on 1007, and all three foot.** **Chapter 429 is day 1038, the thirteenth of Harvestmonth, a Wednesday, and the bar is up and he is paid, and the figure is two hundred and fifty-nine days, which is thirty-seven weeks exactly, which is a hundred and forty-eight up-days, and a hundred and forty-eight less the thirty-seven Sundays is a hundred and eleven paid days, and there are about a hundred and eleven twists of paper in the left pocket of a coat that is not his.** The cross-check is on the page: thirty-one days from Chapter 418 is four weeks and three days, four weeks is sixteen up-days of which twelve are paid, the three of them are two up-days of which one is paid, and about a hundred and thirty and eighteen is about a hundred and forty-eight and about ninety-eight and thirteen is about a hundred and eleven. **A hundred and eleven twists of nine pence is nine hundred and ninety-nine pence, and that is printed outside the day's column and is not added to it, and a mark is forty-eight pence.** The sheet under the stone on the bench in the shed nine feet by seven has been there since the fourteenth of Greenmonth, **about eighty-nine days**, and the bundle behind the stove **about two hundred and nine days**, and the ditch four hundred yards west is the same ditch as Chapter 405 and Chapter 408 and about two hundred carts a week go round a quarter of a mile of it. **The kerb bar and the market bar at Marl were not touched in this block and are named only to be held apart, and the three bars remain three objects.** **The four months of the man of about nineteen came out on the twenty-fourth of a month in Thawmonth and this block did not say it to him and about eight people in that town are right or wrong about it and not one of them has said a word.**

1054. **The one System panel of the volume, and exactly what it said and what it concealed.** `chapter-0426.md`, on the sixth day of Harvestmonth, day 1031, a Wednesday and not a market day, at about the fourth hour, at a house at the end of a lane in the field called Coldharbour, **with a man's left hand flat on a dressed lintel about four feet long with nothing cut on it**, and the hand warm at about the fourth hour, as it is about nine days in ten, and he has never told anybody. **The panel is one line, it is technically true, and it conceals a moral consequence and it does not say what to do: `Every one of the three is true and that is why you cannot do anything with them.`** **It is a warning and it is the volume's only one.** It is not a voice, not a wall and not a man in a room; it is not written in the notebook, and the notebook is eleven lines on a page with no words on any of them and nothing on it about a lintel either. **A System panel is not an explanation and this one explains nothing**: not the mechanism, not the roadmark, not why a man can cross, not what a cut does to an anchor, and not the word in a man's hand. **`stage`, `ledger` and `qualification` are at zero across the ten files and so are `system` and `panel`, and Stage 4 is half-spent, the qualification is not earned and not claimed, and the ledger does not move and no chapter in this block says that it does.**

1055. **The boy of seventeen's arithmetic, what it identified and what it did not open.** `chapter-0427.md`, the eighth of Harvestmonth, day 1033, a Friday and not a market day, at a roadhouse nine miles on past four lime kilns, with a woman of about fifty and about four feet of board with about three hundred and seventy figures on it and no head on it in the kitchen, and a printed slip of four inches by two with the three things on it on the counter for six days. **He cannot read the words and he can count, and he found his own mark in the invented figure because he counts and does not read: the seven in four hundred and seventy is a seven he has been making since he was thirteen, and it got there because the man of about thirty-four turned the boy's own piece of paper over on the counter at the fifth hour of the twenty-fourth of Rainmonth and added the nine figures on the back of it, and a hand cannot be dated and a piece of paper cannot be asked who was standing at the counter.** **The arithmetic he did is the identification and it is an arithmetic and not a crossing: nine entries out of about four hundred carry the mark, the intervals between them counted in days are all the same number, and a road is the only kind of thing a person can count in days, and you can only count one if somebody made it to be gone in a day on purpose and somebody counted it about four hundred times.** It identifies **a road that is nine miles, that is a day's walk, that was shut about four hundred years ago, and that is on no sheet in nine counties.** **Nothing was opened. He has not been down the far half of it, nobody asked him to, nobody in that room thought of it, and there is nothing at the end of it but a field with a gate on it. He counted his own book for the first time in four years and got about four hundred and ten and then about four hundred and said so, and he did not know his own seven-word sentence was seven words.** **Nobody explained the ninth mark, nobody asked him to read the book out loud, and he did not offer.** The two collections are now **ten** marks in common and the tenth is in a file two hundred miles off, and the person who could read the mark is a mason of about sixty-eight about forty miles off who does not know either of them has it.

1056. **What the four days of Chapter 428 cost, and nobody said one word about any of it.** The four days are the ninth, tenth, eleventh and twelfth of Harvestmonth, days 1034 to 1037, a Saturday, a Sunday, a Monday and a Tuesday, and one of the four is a market day, and the four places are an office over a shop, a gate in a field, a yard about two miles from a bar, and a single arch over a beck nine miles off the kilns. **The man of about thirty-four with a sheet wanted somebody's trade against the place and got a remark about a ring. The man of about forty-four who farms Coldharbour wanted him to say the figure was right, because a place with four hundred and seventy on it gets its lane made up and the carts stop going round the quarter of a mile, and he is not lying and the count is honest and about thirty-four people live in that lane. The man of about thirty-three with a barrow wanted the sheet in his coat read and got the reason he had not opened it. A mason of about sixty-eight wanted one sentence carried four miles and got a promise, and his top course is a hand's width out of level and will be a hand's width out of level next spring, and a man of about fifty-one laying a drain arch four miles off is going to be free on the first of a month and nobody has told either of them about the other.** **He did not do any of the four, he did not refuse any of the four, and nobody in any of the four places said one word to him about not doing it, and the block is not a chapter of interior monologue about how hard it was not to do them and nobody in it worked out the moral of anything.**

1057. **The name budget with the arithmetic.** Five for the volume. **Batch 0001 spent three, `Brenna Tull` 44, `Saber Vahl` 68 and `Ivo Serrat` 17, and `Ivo Serrat` was named in Chapter 401 and appears by name twice in this block, in Chapter 423 as one of the nine counters and in Chapter 427 as the boy whose arithmetic is the volume's fifth concrete resolution. Batch 0002 spent none. This block spends none, and two slots remain unfilled for the rest of the volume.** Every other person in the ten chapters is reached by age and by trade: the man of about thirty-four with a sheet, the clerk of about nineteen, the man of about thirty-three with a barrow, the man of about forty-four who farms Coldharbour, the man of about thirty-eight with a crowbar, the man of about nineteen at the bar, the man of about twenty-six who carts salt, the man of about fifty-three who takes loads, the man of about twenty-three in a room over a saddler's, the man of about fifty-three in the yard in 425, the man of about fifty-one at the drain arch, the woman of about fifty at the roadhouse, the woman of about thirty-one with a second-hand stall, the woman of about thirty-one at the stile, the man of about fifty-eight on the bench, the man of about nineteen and about thirty of the farmer's two men, the man of about twenty-two with a handcart, the girl of about thirteen, the man of about twenty at the counter, the man of about fifty-three with eleven dead sheep, and the man of about thirty. **A capitalised two-word sweep over the ten new files, whole word, case-insensitive, headings stripped, every hit read before the sweep was reported, returns `Ivo Serrat` three times and one sentence opening, `Then Aren asked him why`, which is not a proper noun. There is no new name of two words in the block and no new name of one word either.**

1058. **The speech-length figures this block prints, and their footed lengths, counted from the speech as printed with the speaker's attribution excluded.** **Seven printed figures, seven footed, and the word *about* precedes none of them.** `chapter-0422.md` prints **seventeen words** three times for one speech, *It was a harbour once and it is my field now and both of those are true*, and the chapter says the same man said the same seventeen words a fortnight later in `chapter-0426.md`, which is seventeen again. `chapter-0424.md` prints **twelve words** twice for *A trade on a field is a licence and not a description*, **and the block's own prompt said nine words for that sentence, which is a card figure that does not reproduce and the sentence on the page is the card's own sentence counted.** `chapter-0426.md` prints **nine words** for *The ditch takes my barrow down about two inches* and **seventeen** for the farmer's sentence. `chapter-0427.md` prints **seven words** for *I have got that mark nine times* and the first printing said nine, which was wrong by two and is item 1049. `chapter-0428.md` prints **ten words** for *Will you ask him whether he would take it on*, **and the first printing had *about ten words to say* against a speech the page prints, which the prompt's §5 item 12 forbids.** `chapter-0429.md` prints **fifteen words** for *I do not know and I cannot read and nobody has ever asked me either.* **No figure attached to a speech this block prints is unfooted, and one reference to a figure in a prior chapter was cut for the reason in item 1049 because Chapter 413 prints no forty-four-word speech.**

1059. **The money columns, footed in pence on the page before the total was said, and the totals.** **Ten columns, ten footed, ten distinct totals: 40, 33, 29, 38, 42, 45, 48, 47, 144 and 34 pence**, in Chapters 421 to 430 in order. **None is one of the forty recurring totals in the block prompt's §4 and none is one of Batch 0001's four or Batch 0002's ten at 16, 22, 17, 3, 112, 135, 70, 31, 56 and 70, and the four barred numerals `thirty-six` `fifty-two` `sixty-one` `sixty-six` are at zero across the ten files.** A mark is forty-eight pence in every conversion on the page: forty is under one, thirty-three is about two-thirds, thirty-eight is about four-fifths, forty-two is under one, forty-five is under one, forty-seven is a penny under one, forty-eight is a mark exactly, one hundred and forty-four is three marks exactly, thirty-four is about two-thirds, and one shilling and four pence is sixteen pence and four shillings is forty-eight pence. **The bar's own money, nine hundred and ninety-nine pence, is printed outside Chapter 429's day's column and is added to nothing, and the two are not in one column.** **No rate is paid in salt anywhere in the block and no conversion was required and none is printed.** **And the two prices of the same thing are never added in any register, including the notch in Chapter 429 and the pair of barrow-loads of glass that is the same glass.**

1060. **The checks, each on a named basis, and what each one returned, and the one that returned a false negative and is re-issued here.** **The calendar, by derivation, twice, forward from the single anchor of day 930 on the carried thirty-day month, on both halves of every dateline and every dated reference inside the ten chapters: twenty-eight checks, and the first printing of this item reported them as zero mismatches after the eight Harvestmonth datelines were corrected, and that report was false.** **The day-of-month half was zero mismatches and the weekday half was seven, and the check reported one number for two independent derivations and the number it reported was the one that had already been repaired. A check that returns a single figure for two derivations is not two derivations. Both halves are now returned separately and are zero mismatches each, and the seven weekday corrections are item 1049 defect eleven and the repair is item 1063.** The rest of the item stands as printed and was re-run after the last edit of this pass. **The money, read on the page and not from a state file: ten columns, ten footed in pence, ten distinct totals, zero collisions with the forty recurring totals and zero with the ten Batch 0002 columns, and each column read against the column above it and not only against the total below it, which is what caught Chapter 423's nine items summing to twenty-five against a printed twenty-nine.** **Speech-length figures, counted speech by speech from the printed words with the speaker's attribution excluded: seven printed, seven of seven foot, none preceded by *about*, and one uncounted figure found by the block's own audit and now foot and now on the page.** **Raw-token, headings stripped, bold datelines in, fifteen words or more, maximal-run basis and not a sliding window: the ten new against all four hundred and twenty prior return, at Chapter 424, twenty-nine words, which is `And` plus the declared refrain *a toll is not a wage and a wage is not a toll and a bar is not a rate and a return is not a toll* and is declared and not re-voiced; the rest are the dateline frame at fifteen to nineteen words, declared. The ten new against themselves return nothing above twenty words, and the longest is the framer of two money columns, declared in kind.** **Nine runs of twenty-eight words or more against prior chapters were found in the first printing and all nine were re-voiced: the bar's three figures and the coat and the twists in Chapter 429 against `chapter-0418.md` and `chapter-0405.md`, the bar dateline in 429 against the same two, the bar's money line in 429 against `chapter-0418.md`, the notebook in Chapter 426 against `chapter-0416.md`, the notebook's figures in Chapter 427 against the same, the roadhouse in Chapter 427 against `chapter-0408.md`, the nine feet of dressed top course in Chapter 428 against `chapter-0416.md`, the ninth line and the nine words in Chapter 430 against `chapter-0402.md`, `chapter-0407.md` and `chapter-0419.md`, the eight words in Chapter 425 against `chapter-0414.md`, and the drained creek and the ditch in Chapter 426 against `chapter-0405.md` and `chapter-0408.md`.** **Exact duplicate paragraphs, whitespace-normalised, twelve words or more, the ten new against the whole manuscript and against themselves: none, and the scope is the whole manuscript as it stood at the time of the run.** **Per-line bold parity even on every line of every file; `---` rules 0; double blank lines 0; a missing-full-stop sweep 0; a space-after-a-comma sweep 0; a space-before-a-full-stop sweep 0; a doubled-word sweep 0; a lowercase-word-into-a-capitalised-word sweep 0.** **The twelve barred tokens and the four barred numerals, whole word, every hit read and not only counted, at zero, and with `stage` `ledger` `qualification` `system` `panel` at zero.** **A self-reference sweep across nineteen patterns testing the bare noun: `this volume`, `this book`, `this chapter`, `this block`, `this batch`, `this manuscript`, `this narrator`, `about four hundred chapters`, `the whole of this` and the three retired reporting-frame forms are all at zero. `on the page` returns 1, `the page` 1, `the book` 4 and `in that book` 1, and all seven are world objects and every one was read: a page under a fourth step, a form, a notebook of eleven lines, a book of about four hundred entries a boy made himself, and a tenant's book.** **A figure audit, read against the chapter and against the chapter three away: the three invented days 1017, 1019 and 1020 are the same three days in Chapters 422, 423, 424, 425, 427 and 430; the count spans 1028's fourth hour to 1029's tenth hour and Chapter 423 is datelined the twenty-fourth; the bar runs 170 on 949, 193 on 972, 228 on 1007 and 259 on 1038; the sheet under the stone runs 58 days on 1007 and about 89 on 1038; the fold in the coat runs about 8 days on 1007, 37 on 1036 and 38 on 1038; the bundle runs about 120 on 949, about 178 on 1007 and about 209 on 1038; and the market leak is 57 days before Chapter 430.**

1061. **And what Batch 0003 did not do, which is the standing instruction for Batch 0004 and is not a list of omissions.** It did not name the antagonist, and it did not revive the Volume 08 cooperation, and nothing in it is a reconciliation or a reunion. It did not print a fifth unattributed two-line statement, the budget being four of four and spent. It did not print the word that names the founding paper in any construction. It did not mention a tile on a coping, schedule anything for one, or print the word *roof*. It did not add an enemy, an office, a council, an order, a mechanism or a person with a want about any of this. **It did not make any of the three invented things a lie and it did not make any of them a forgery, and there is no villain to catch and nobody is brought to anything, and the thing is not corrected inside the block and the protagonist does not stop it and says no word about not having stopped it.** It did not have anybody find the bench-board or read it, and the ninth line is reported as still ruled and empty and nobody is near it. It did not reconcile the refusal figures, add any two of them, or add any of them to a figure of persons. It did not move the count on the page under the fourth step and it did not put a tenth asking to that bench, and the day's book and the register are untouched. It did not print the marginal instruction, the count of the looks at a leaf, the figure of persons against a district, or the number of persons the office will not print on an offer. It did not explain the mechanism, make a cut, schedule one, or name the works under the abbey, and there is one System panel and no second. It did not resolve the shut road four hundred miles off, the offer, the about eleven hundred, the two villages, the corridor's rule or its rate, or the want of either woman of about twenty-nine or thirty-nine, and it did not restate either want and nobody is thanked. It did not take Hushwater and printed neither the near-sixty nor the eighty. It did not merge the three bars. It did not print the corridor's rate as a figure. It did not answer the question from a road and did not have the keeper of records answer it. It did not soften the leak, un-say the ninth word, or have anybody regret it in a speech, and it did not reprint the twenty-word engine sentence of Chapter 407. It did not destroy or withdraw the register, repeal the quarantine, or adjourn the hearing. **And it did not spend Ivo Serrat's arithmetic before the identification was made, did not point at the mason's mark, did not have anybody explain the ninth mark, and did not have anybody ask the boy to read his book out loud.**

1062. **And what Batch 0004 must not do, in one list, which is item 1062 and not a summary of the eleven above.** **Do not name the antagonist. Do not print a third of the barred kin-term, which is at 2 across four hundred and thirty chapters and is spent. Do not print a fifth unattributed two-line statement. Do not print the word that names the founding paper. Do not print a tenth line of the song, do not un-say the ninth word, do not soften the leak and do not have anybody regret it in a speech. Do not print a second System panel, a stage word, a ledger word or a qualification word. Do not resolve the shut road four hundred miles off, the offer, the about eleven hundred, the two villages, the corridor's rule or its rate, or the want of either woman of about twenty-nine or thirty-nine, and do not answer the offer with a figure. Do not answer the question from a road and do not have the keeper of records answer it. Do not repair the breach in a scene and do not report the span as a repair. Do not reopen the city, the valley, the press's opening day, the roadmark, the mechanism, the leaf with a seventh line that says *a person*, either line in a stone, the marginal instruction, the word in front of a place name, or the account of the worst winter in eighteen years. Do not merge the three bars and do not touch the kerb bar or the market bar at Marl. Do not take Hushwater, and the near-sixty and the eighty may not be printed together or compared on a page. Do not add a tenth asking of that bench, a tenth not-asking, or an eighth register entry. Do not print the corridor's rate as a figure. Do not schedule the seed vault, the bell's device, a second oath-stone renewal, or Chapter 900. **Do not correct the three invented things, do not make any of them a lie, do not remove any of them, and do not have anybody find the bench-board, read it, or improve on it. Do not have the protagonist overrule anybody. Do not let him print a word about the four days he spent not doing four things, and do not let him work out the moral of anything. And do not improve on a want.**"

## Volume 09, Batch 0003 — repair pass after review, on the eleventh of a review

**This section was written by a repair phase and not by the phase that wrote the block. It changed eight files and it restarted nothing. It resolved the calendar and nothing else, and it resolved nothing the block left open on purpose. The plot is unchanged, the three invented things are unchanged, the fourth unpaid thing of Chapter 428 is unchanged, the bench-board is still ruled and empty, the System panel is still spent, and the antagonist is still named nowhere.**

1063. **The seven wrong weekday labels, what each one cost in prose, and what was and was not touched.** **Four chapters and seven labels, and the day of month and the day number were right in every case, so no chapter was moved in the calendar and nothing was renumbered.** Chapter 425 is day 1029, a Monday and not a market day, and it is the only chapter the defect cost anything structural to. It had been built on a market morning, and its fourth section put the protagonist in a room where a bench sits on Saturdays, and its fifth section had him walk nine miles on a Sunday and stand at a gate on the Monday morning. **Three things in one chapter depended on it being a Saturday, and all three are repaired without moving a day: the market-hour claim in the first section became a Monday claim, and the two hundred people who had heard one of the three became about a hundred and forty, and the bench scene moved to the Saturday before, which is day 1027, the morning the band of about a hundred and four sheets went out, and he is in that town on both days and is in the yard on neither.** **The walk is now Tuesday into Wednesday and the gate is a Wednesday morning, and Chapter 426 opens on a Wednesday having walked on a Tuesday, so the two chapters now agree with each other, and before the repair they agreed with each other and not with the chain, which is the only reason the defect survived a review that read both.** Chapter 426's own cost column described a bed taken in a barn on a Sunday and now describes Tuesday, and Chapter 427's dateline said the slip had been on the counter five days when the Saturday it came in on is day 1027 and the chapter is day 1033, which is six. **Chapter 428's four days were relabelled in the dateline and in all four section heads, and its claim that two of the four were market days became one, because 1034 is the only Saturday of the four.**

1064. **Four state-file defects found by the same reading, three of which the review did not name.** **One, the market-morning count.** Continuity 1048 and `chapter-summaries.md` and `open-threads.md` 19 all said *eight of the ten chapter days are in Harvestmonth*, and it is **five of ten**, being four days of Rainmonth, five of Harvestmonth and one of Fallowmonth, and eight is the count of the thirteen datelines in Harvestmonth and the two numbers were conflated. **The same item said *five of the ten chapters fall on a market morning and five do not* and it is two, and it said the count at the stile begins on day 1028 when 1028 is a day of Harvestmonth and the dateline is the twenty-third of Rainmonth, which is day 1018.** **Two, the certification.** `state/current.md` and this file's item 1060 both stated that all thirty datelines across three blocks foot on both halves with zero mismatches, derived twice, and that was the claim that let the defect through, and it is withdrawn and replaced with the separate half-by-half result. **Three, the next prompt.** `workspace/volume-09/batch-0004/PROMPT.md` told the writer that the whole of Block 3 was in Harvestmonth, and §2 named only one direction of the calendar error, and §7 item 1 asked for both halves of every dateline without saying that a check returning one number for two derivations is one derivation. **All four are corrected, and item 1062's list of what Batch 0004 must not do is untouched and is not amended by this pass, because a repair pass is not where a plan is changed.**

1065. **And what this pass did not do, which is half of a repair and the half that is usually left out.** **It did not touch a single money column: all ten foot in pence, the totals are still 40, 33, 29, 38, 42, 45, 48, 47, 144 and 34, all ten are distinct, and none collides with the forty recurring totals or with Batch 0002's ten.** It did not touch a quotation, so all seven printed speech-length figures are still footed at seventeen, twelve, nine, seventeen, seven, ten and fifteen and none is preceded by *about*. The bar's arithmetic in Chapter 429 is untouched and still foots at 259 days, 148 up-days, 111 paid days and 999 pence. **The twelve barred tokens and the five stage words are still at zero, the retired reporting frame is still at zero, the self-reference sweep across nineteen patterns is still at zero, and the twelve barred tokens across the whole manuscript still stand where item 1048's neighbours put them.** **The block grew from 16,904 words to 16,970 and the manuscript from 1,348,869 to 1,348,935 in four hundred and thirty chapters, and the `about` count moved from 489 to 490 and the rate from 28.93 to 28.87 per 1,000, and all three figures are recounted on the chapter files with the title line in and not read off this item.** **The only prose this pass added is the bridge in Chapter 425's fourth section and the weekday corrections in the same chapter's first and fifth, and it added no scene, no speech, no character, no name and no fact about the world, and it took nothing out of the block except a number that was wrong.**

## Volume 09 Batch 0004, Chapters 431 to 440

**This section is written by the phase that wrote Block 0004 and it continues from 1065. Every figure was counted on the chapter files as they stand after the block's own repair pass and the basis is named. Nothing above is renumbered and nothing above is withdrawn.**

1066. **The frame, derived twice from the single anchor, and the two halves reported as two numbers.** Day 930, the twenty-fifth of Mudmonth YR 318, a Sunday, on the carried thirty-day month, is the only anchor; Mudmonth 1 YR 318 is day 906, and the month boundaries inside the volume are 936, 966, 996, 1026, 1056, 1086 and 1116. **The ten chapter days are 1057, 1057 and 1058, 1062, 1068, 1071, 1074, 1078, 1080, 1084 and 1098, which are the second and third days of Fallowmonth, the seventh, the thirteenth, the sixteenth, the nineteenth, the twenty-third, the twenty-fifth and the twenty-ninth of Fallowmonth, and the thirteenth of Embermonth, and they are a Monday, a Monday and a Tuesday, a Saturday, a Friday, a Monday, a Thursday, a Monday, a Wednesday, a Sunday and a Sunday.** The weekday half and the day-of-month half were derived separately and returned separately, and **both are zero mismatches: the weekday half over ten datelines and the day-of-month half over eleven dated references, each from the day number alone, and the market-morning claim in all eleven is zero mismatches from the day number alone as well.** The block's own chapter days run 1057 to 1098, forty-two days inclusive, inside the prompt's window. **Two of the ten chapters fall on a market morning, being Chapter 433 on the Saturday the seventh of Fallowmonth and Chapter 436 on the Thursday the nineteenth.** The year does not turn in this block and no month name spoken in a body fails to resolve to a day: `Fallowmonth` resolves to 1056, `Rainmonth` to 996, `Harvestmonth` to 1026, and `Wolfmonth` in Chapter 438 is spoken twice with the words *of the year before* attached and is the bar's own start at day 779, which is YR 317. **`outline/volume-09.md` §17's rows for 1098 and 1099 are one day out and were not copied: this block's last day is 1098, a Sunday and not a market day, and Block 5 begins on 1099, a Monday.** The 965 row is recorded at 1047 and the 1098 and 1099 rows are recorded at `state/open-threads.md` 19.

1067. **The four men, what they took, and the receipt, and who wrote it.** **Four men on a strength with a paper carrying a seal and a name on it, and no lock on the door to break, and the man of about thirty-eight with a crowbar who lifted the door off its sill in about a second and a half and put the bar back on its bracket inside two minutes.** They took **about forty-one sheets off the top board of a nine-foot shelf** between the third hour and the seventh hour of the second of Fallowmonth, into a satchel on a handcart, and **they counted it twice, off the shelf in three lots at nineteen, fifteen and seven, and into the satchel in three lots at twenty-two, thirteen and six, and both counts foot to forty-one, and all six figures are different from one another.** **Nothing was burnt, nothing was read, nothing was said that was not true, and the four men asked nobody anything.** The receipt was written by **the man of about thirty-four with a sheet, in a fair hand, on the back of the sealed paper because the front of that paper was not his to write on**, and it was left **under the door of that building with the door off its sill**, and it says what was taken, where it was taken from, on which days, and by whom, and then a line underneath to say what the thing was, **and the thing it says is copied off the last line in the day-book, in the same order, a place, a figure of men and a trade.** It is accurate, it is the volume's second receipt, and a receipt is a record and it cannot be given back. **On the twenty-third he wrote a second line under the first that said the building was cleared and did not say what went out of it, and that second line is accurate in the same way.** Nobody in nine counties can say who ordered it, and the volume does not say, and the reason it does not say is printed on the page in Chapter 439: a boy of seventeen worked out from three numbers that the four men did not know what was on the shelf, and a man who does not know what is on a shelf is not a man who has been told what is in it, and the man who sent them is a person who did not think about a shelf at all.

1068. **The building, its slate, its last keeper and the winter she died in.** **A two-room building of about twenty-two feet by eleven at the back of a paved yard behind a house of assizes in a town of about nine hundred people, built of the same stone as the yard wall and about four hundred years older than it.** The company that had it wound up about nine years ago, and a woman went on keeping it for about seven years after that, on her own, until she died in a winter, **and the office that could appoint a keeper does not know the building exists, because a schedule is a list of what a court is answerable for and nobody has been answerable for it in four hundred years.** There are nine things on the clerk's schedule and every one of them is a thing the court is answerable for. **The door has no lock and has not had one since before she died, and the reason is rust and a stone and nobody buying another, and that is the ordinary way a door comes to have no lock.** The door had dropped about half an inch on a dressed sill stone about four feet long, and it weighs more than two men and a barrow. **A slate about two feet by one foot is set in the yard wall six feet off the corner of the building at the height of a man's shoulder, with a name cut on it in letters an inch high, and two more lines cut half the depth of the first, and the second of the two is a month, a day and a year, and there is no line under them for anything else, and about nine people in that town could tell you what is cut there, and the man of thirty-two with a limp did not say the name out loud to a stranger.** The last line anybody wrote in the day-book is the fourth of a month in a winter two winters back, and it is the last of about four hundred and some lines.

1069. **The eleven witnesses, in their eleven places, with the day each found out and who told them, and the two who were handed a copy and did not have to be told.** **The first, a woman of about forty-four who runs the second kiln of four in a yard of four kilns two days west of Merefen, on the seventh of Fallowmonth, day 1062, a Saturday and a market day, told nine times in four days by a man of about forty with a cart of lime at the gate, a woman of about thirty-four on her own doorstep, a man of about fifty who buys stone in the middle of an argument, and six more, of whom three said the same as the first, two said it wrong and one added a thing that came from a boy at a well.** **The second, a man of about forty-one who walks a boundary of two miles and nine stones, on the thirteenth, day 1068, told by the man with the stick, who asked him a question and got the answer a boundary gives, in twenty-one words.** **The third, a mason of about sixty-eight, on the sixteenth, day 1071, told by a man of about twenty-nine who carts lime and had been told by a boy at a well.** **The fourth, a woman of about fifty who has kept a roadhouse nine miles on from the kilns for thirty-one years, told on the nineteenth, day 1074, and handed a copy of the receipt on the second, because she is the only person on eleven miles of that road who hands a stranger a written thing when a stranger pays for something.** **The fifth, a woman of about thirty-seven who keeps a roadhouse eleven miles on from the yard and hangs a plate out on a bracket over her door, given a copy on the second and found out on the twenty-fifth, day 1080.** **The sixth, a man of about thirty-three with a barrow, given a copy on the second and found out on the twenty-fifth, and it is the only time in about three months he has looked at anything anybody handed him.** **The seventh, a man of about nineteen at a bar four miles west of a market town of about two thousand people on the salt, given a copy on the made road on the second and found out on the twenty-fifth, and it is in the inside of his coat and he cannot read it.** **The eighth, a man of about twenty-six who carts salt and counts
